APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

183

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Brethren, I would be remiss if this report was not prefaced by
expressing my sincere gratitude for the opportunity and privilege of serving you
and this Grand Lodge as its Grand Lecturer.
One of the duties of this office is to give an account of services
rendered during the year and to set forth, with respect to ritual, the conditions of
the Lodges. With that duty in mind, I offer for your consideration, the following
report.
Several years ago, a former Inspector, the late James E. Bacon wrote
this statement:
“The ritual is the basis of all our Masonic work. While Masonic
achievement must never end with the ritual, it must always begin there.
Upon the quality of the beginning depends the success of the ending,
and we should always insist upon absolute, flawless and assiduous
accuracy”.
Every part of our ritual should be done with this statement in mind.
There are times when we hear the statement, “We put on a first degree”, or a
second or third degree and in many cases, that is probably a true statement,
rather than conferring a meaningful degree or lesson to the candidate. When a
candidate’s application is received, he has expressed a desire and made an
investment to be a part of our Fraternity. After his acceptance, it is our
responsibility to provide him with the best possible instructions, not just the
conferral of the three degrees, but to provide further knowledge that he may
benefit from the principles of Freemasonry and become a better man to his
family and an asset to his Lodge and community. Ask yourselves, “Are we
providing the best possible instructions or are we just going through the required
steps and motions?” What goals have been established, or do we have any?
Many Lodges are continuing to struggle with member participation to
serve as officers. In many situations, Past Masters are “stepping up to the plate”
to serve in the various stations so their Lodge may remain viable and function in
accordance with our Masonic Code. We appreciate their dedication and desire
to help their Lodge. Other Lodges not only suffer from lack of officers, but,
don’t seem to have the desire to work harmoniously with their membership.
Some members seem to have forgotten the basic principles taught them.
Individual agendas seem to destroy that “teamwork concept” of “who best can
work and best agree”.
While these situations do exist, other Lodges are receiving applications,
for the degrees, in significant numbers and I am pleased to report that many
candidates are young men, some even desiring to learn the long form

184

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

proficiencies. They are desirous in seeking further knowledge in Masonry that
they may become better husbands, better fathers and better citizens in their
communities. These positive attitudes do exist and your Ritual Committee
embraces their enthusiasm.
While many Lodges are struggling with various problems, some of
these same Lodges have outstanding community oriented programs, such as:
Public Schools Recognition and Scholarships; Child I.D. Programs; Fair
Information Booths; Local Parade Participation; Highway Right Away Trash
Clean-up and other community activities. As Lodges are participating in any
community oriented activity, they are maintaining their own Masonic identity,
and creating a unified Masonic environment. We applaud these community
involved Lodges and encourage their continued efforts.
For nine years, our Jurisdiction had been divided into (4) four
Divisions. This year another Division was added which required a different
geographical alignment to balance the Jurisdiction into (5) five Divisions. Other
factors entered into this change, such as the impact it might have on finances.
After analyzing previous years expenses and the Grand Lodge’s financial
position, your Grand Lecturer made the decision to consolidate the Inspectors
Spring Conferences, locating one in the North and one in the South. The
Northern Conference consisting of Divisions I, II and V was held in Merced.
The Southern Conference consisting of Divisions III and IV was held in Buena
Park. This arrangement reflected a considerable decrease in cost, for these
Conferences, over prior years of individual Division Conferences, but did not
take anything away from the ritual exemplification and review. There were
added benefits by allowing a larger number of Inspectors to participate and work
together. Most of the Inspectors were pleased with this arrangement and support
its continuance.
This year a goal was set for each Division to have at least (2) two Ritual
Seminars for Officers Coaches and (2) two Ritual Seminars for Master Masons.
The goal was to exemplify all three degrees. We must continue to concentrate
and improve our ritual in its entirety. Setting our goals high, with determination,
will produce those desired results. The Officers Coaches were most grateful and
enthused and requested more seminars. The Master Masons Seminars received a
similar response. Each seminar was well attended taking into consideration the
geographical locations.
At the 2001 Annual Communication, Grand Master Decker’s
Recommendation No. 4 was approved by the delegates of this Grand Lodge.
The scope of this recommendation called for the appointment of a task force to
write a ritual manual as a supplement to our adopted cipher ritual, which would
provide directions and instructions for the conferral of our degrees. The
composition of the task force consisted of your Ritual Committee and others
skilled in our ritual. Considerable time and concentration has gone into this
challenge. Research of other jurisdictions, that have cipher rituals with various

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

185

instructions, have been sought and analyzed. Considerable progress has been
made through the diligent and conscientious efforts of all involved. The status,
of this assignment, is nearing completion of the initial draft, both narrative and
illustrative, however, much critique and testing remains to be accomplished
before making a final presentation. Therefore, further time is required. I extend
my sincere appreciation to my colleagues, the Assistant Grand Lecturers, and all
others who have dedicated so much time and effort for this task.
Your Ritual Committee is most appreciative to all the Lodges who have
acquired and complied with the latest 2001 revision of the Installation Ceremony
for Constituent Lodges and the Book of Ceremonies. These are available in the
Grand Lodge Supply Room and we encourage each Lodge to have them.
In conclusion, my heartfelt gratitude is extended to Most Worshipful C.
Ray Whitaker and his wife Joyce, for being a part of their Grand Lodge Family.
His dedicated leadership and kind friendship will always be remembered. I
extend my sincere appreciation to our Grand Secretary, and his most capable
staff, for all their assistance and cooperation. To my colleagues, the Assistant
Grand Lecturers, their continued cooperation and dedication is appreciated more
than what one can describe. Each one has served with distinction and is a true
asset to this Committee. It has been a pleasure serving with each of them. To
each of the Inspectors, a simple “Thank you” seems so inadequate, but it does
come from the heart. One really does not know the real importance of their
duties, unless he has served in that position. All your labors are greatly
appreciated. And to you, my Brethren, thank you for your friendship and kind
support. We are all part of a team, laboring together. Remember, “Nothing is
insurmountable, unless we allow it to be so”. It is not what I can do or you can
do as an individual, but, what we can do together in harmony.
Thank you again, for allowing me the opportunity to serve as your
Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted.
Harold A. Macy, Grand Lecturer

APPENDIX G

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

189

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend Thomas M. Boles
“THE BEAUTY OF WORSHIP”
“What a beautiful day the Lord hath made. Let us rejoice and be glad
in it.”
I’d like to share a little story with you about this minister that waited in
line to have his car filled with gas just before a long holiday weekend. The
attendant worked quickly but there were many cars ahead of him. Finally, the
attendant motioned him toward a vacant pump. “Reverend,” said the young
attendant, “sorry about the delay. It seems as if everyone waits until the last
minute to get ready for a long trip.” The minister chuckled, “I know what you
mean. It’s the same in my business.”
Well, I just wanted to remind you of the value of going to your house of
worship at least once in a while.
You know, “To worship is to quicken the conscience by the holiness of
God, to purge the imagination by the beauty of God, to open the heart to the love
of God, and to devote your will to the purpose of God.”
Yes, to worship is to experience reality, to touch life. It is to know, to
feel, to experience the presence of the Supreme Architect in the midst of the
gathered community. It is a breaking into the shekinah of God, or better yet,
being invaded by the skekinah of God, or the presence of God in your life.
It says in the holy writings that the true worshiper will worship the
Father in spirit and truth, for such, the Father seeks to worship Him. It is God
who seeks, draws, persuades. Worship is the human response to the divine
initiative.
Scripture is replete with examples of God’s efforts to initiate, restore
and maintain fellowship with His children. God is like the father of the prodigal
who upon seeing his son a long way off, rushed to welcome him home.
I believe sometimes we forget just why we worship! Worship is our
response to the overtures of love from the heart of the Father. Its central reality
is found in spirit and truth. It is kindled within us only when the spirit of God
touches our human spirit. Forms and rituals do not produce worship, nor does
the disuse of forms and rituals. We can use all the right techniques and methods,
we can have the best possible liturgy, but we have not worshiped the Lord until
spirit touches spirit. The words of the chorus, “Set my spirit free that I may
worship Thee,” reveals the basis of worship.
Until God touches and frees our spirit we cannot enter his realm.
Singing, praying, praising all may lead to worship, but worship is more than any

190

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

of them. Our spirit must be ignited by the divine fire, that the light within us
may burn brighter and brighter.
As a result, we need not be overly concerned with the question of a
correct form for worship. The issue of high liturgy or low liturgy, this form or
that form is peripheral rather than the center. So, why do we, individually, get so
concerned about what religion a person belongs to, or what denomination they
belong to? We sometimes forget that nowhere does the holy writings prescribe a
particular form for worship. In fact, what we find is a freedom that is incredible
for all people, even those with such deep roots in a particular religion. When
spirit touches spirit the issue of forms is wholly secondary.
To say that forms are secondary is not to say that they are irrelevant.
As long as we are finite human beings we must have forms of some kind. We
must have “wineskins” that will embody our experience of worship. But the
forms are not the worship; they only lead us into the worship. We are free in the
choice we make, to use whatever forms will enhance our worship and if any form
hinders us from experiencing the living God, well – too bad for the form.
We are told in the holy writings “To worship the Lord ‘YOUR’ God,
and him only shall you serve.” As I tell my congregation at the Midnight
Mission in Los Angeles, I want them, at all times, to worship the God of their
choice, not mine. It’s strange, however, but by any name, we seem to always be
worshipping the same “Supreme Being”.
We desperately need to see who God is; to read about His selfdisclosure to His ancient people, to mediate on His attributes, to gaze upon the
revelation of His nature.
If the Lord is to be Lord, worship must have priority in our lives. The
divine priority is worship first and service second. Our lives are to be
punctuated with praise, thanksgiving and adoration.
Service flows out of worship! Service as a substitute for worship is
idolatry. Activity is the enemy of adoration. One grave temptation we all face is
to run around answering calls to service without first ministering to the Lord
himself.
I wish, in this regard, that when we Freemasons encounter a concern,
we first minister to the Lord. I’m sure the problems and concerns will diminish
much quicker!
Today, God is calling His children back to worship. This can be seen
in high church circles where there is a renewed interest in intimacy with God.
And it can be seen in low church circles where there is renewed interest in
liturgy. And it can be seen everywhere in between these two.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

191

It is as if God is saying, “I want the hearts of my people back!” And if
we long to go where God is going and do what God is doing, we will move into
deeper, more authentic worship.
I guess my point is that worship is a deliberate and disciplined
adventure in reality. It is not for the timid or comfortable. It involves an
opening of ourselves to the adventurous life of the spirit. It makes all the
religious paraphernalia of temples and priests and rites and ceremonies
irrelevant. It involves a willingness to “let the word of the Lord richly dwell in
us, as you teach and admonish one another in all wisdom, and as with
thankfulness in our hears to God.”
Therefore, today, let us begin a new and refreshing approach to our
worship of the Supreme Being of your choice.
Let us pray: O God, our true and highest life, by whom, through whom,
and in whom all things live, which live truly and blessedly, we humbly pray Thee
so to guide and govern us by Thy holy spirit, that in all the cares and occupations
of our daily life we may never forget Thee, but remember that we are ever
walking in Thy sight. To Thy holy presence we pray. Amen.

192

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

APPENDIX H

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

195

REPORT OF THE GRAND ORATOR
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, distinguished and
honored guests, fellow Grand Lodge officers, and my Brethren all.
Seven score and thirteen years ago, there assembled in California
honorable men of vision and courage. Men of great wisdom and even greater
compassion. These men conceived our great fraternity in California.
But what came them here to do? What came you here to do?
In 1849, a mere 69 men, so inspired by the impassioned cries of their
families and friends did raise funds.
They raised $4,500 to comfort the sick—the equivalent of nearly $8
million today or more than $100,000 per Mason. Those California Masons
buried the dead, and built a house of healing for those in distress for $32,000—
the equivalent of over $50 million today or more than three-quarters of a million
dollars per Mason, and they provided for that hospital’s financial support over
two years of anguish during California’s cholera epidemic.
These compassionate souls were truly Freemasons, and our Masonic
history is filled with similar acts of kindness and charity to widows and orphans,
and Brothers of the Craft, whether they be members of a California Lodge or
sojourners.
Did these men hold the special honor of being the only men of
compassion and valor in so dark a time? Were Freemasons the sole claim
holders to these selfless acts? To suggest this would reek of self-importance and
boastful pride. We, each one of us know of, or belong to other such worthy
institutions. Hence, charity is not the true purpose of our Craft.
No, it was not these selfless acts that defined the purpose of these
Masons. These were merely demonstrations of the works and deeds of good
men, and Freemasonry has no corner on the market for such men. Everyone of us
knows of men of such caliber and quality.
For as long as anyone of us can remember, our fraternity purports to
take good men and make them better. This is a noble posture for an organization.
But is it unique? Are we the only organization that can claim this worthy task?
Are there no others that can accomplish this important endeavor?
To disallow all other organizations from this important work would be
folly. Among those engaged in this role are the organized religions of the world.
Those organizations of worship to Deity would remind us that they were charged
by the Great Architect of the Universe with this duty as part of their primary role
in society. Hence, it is not even logical that this is our true purpose.
So what came we here to do?

196

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

I, myself, did not come intentionally to improve myself in Masonry and
learn to subdue my passions. Did you? Can any of us honestly say that we knew
we were good men and only needed to become better? Can any of us truly say
that we believed that if we reached out to join a Lodge we would become better?
Did we come here to join an organization because we were looking for
a venue to perform our charitable works? Possibly for some, who became
motivated by the various public expressions of our charitable endeavors.
Did you and I join as a result of a need for more members in our
Lodges? Are we the result of a membership drive? More members; for what
purpose? To fill what need? Were we trying to grow our membership to spread
the word? What word?
What is the purpose of growing our membership? Are we wanting men
to join because we need more dues revenue to support the bricks and mortar of
the fraternity? Are our membership motives driven by a desire to support a
complex infrastructure which evolved over many decades? Or, are we no longer
interested in spending time with those brothers currently belonging to our
Lodges? Are we a multi-level marketing organization that requires the constant
influx of new members to support those on the level above?
We seem to be more of an assembly line that has been experiencing a
slow down in production, rather than a fraternal society of friends and brothers
who desire to spend time which each other. We seem content to measure our
success and importance by the number of bodies moved through the assembly
line, rather than the quality of those men and their satisfaction with the outcomes
or final product.
Have we forgotten why we joined this fraternity?
I had, until I spent the last twelve months traveling this fantastic state. I
had the opportunity to experience a transformation in my passion for this
fraternity. I had the opportunity to experience the value of the many friendships
that come with a membership in this fraternity.
Yes, I thoroughly enjoyed delivering orations for 30 cornerstone and
rededication ceremonies. To deliver messages to children and parents about the
importance of education, and to touch the hearts of firefighters engaged in our
personal safety, were some of the most meaningful moments.
I have enjoyed the honor bestowed upon me by the most worshipful C.
Ray Whitaker when he selected me as the Grand Orator for this year. More
importantly, Ray is a Brother who also became a friend of mine.
Yes, I did enjoy representing our fraternity in the various communities
to which we traveled. I only hope you have enjoyed the messages I prepared and
which I believed were most appropriate for each event audience.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

197

But more than anything else, I enjoyed the opportunity to be reminded,
for twelve months, about why I joined this fraternity. It was what I loved the
most about this year.
I made new friendships and rekindled old ones. I spent time with people
I truly enjoyed.
That is why I joined this fraternity. I wanted to spend time with people
who had similar values to mine, similar interests to me, and people who trust me
as much as I trust them.
This isn’t a recruiting point. It doesn’t require a fancy building to
accomplish. It requires spending time with the people who are meaningful to you
and me.
It doesn’t require hundreds of thousands of people; it requires hundreds
of thousands of moments to share with people we want to be with.
I wish for each of you to look within your hearts and remember why
you joined.
Could you not perform charitable works without Freemasonry? Could
you not improve yourself without Freemasonry?
Or, didn’t you join because you wanted to be with like-minded people?
Didn’t you join because you wanted to be with friends? Didn’t you join because
of the opportunities to meet more people who share common values?
There is nothing more valuable in our lives than time; no jewel more
precious. Our days are numbered, and that is what gives them value. We must
choose wisely how we spend this most valuable resource.
More than thirteen years ago, I chose to spend some amount of my time
in this fraternity. I wanted to experience the enjoyment that my father-in-law had
experienced in Freemasonry.
I met the members of my lodge and wanted to spend time with them. I
have always been a community volunteer, and I did not need to learn how to be
philanthropic. I desired to be with men who would want to spend time with me.
I see now, more clearly than ever, that whether I knew it consciously or
not, I was joining to be with you.
Each of us sets our priorities in life. We spend time on those things that
are most meaningful to us. This does not mean that we spend time on the most
urgent or most important. It means we choose to spend our time on those things
that are most meaningful, and of the greatest perceived value.
When only ten percent of my lodge is the average attendance at
meetings, then I know that ninety percent perceive these meetings as less

198

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

meaningful and of lesser value than whatever activity they are engaged in at that
time.
This does not devalue me, nor does it mean that they are wrong in not
attending. It means that we no longer fulfill the needs of those brethren.
To survive we must build upon those reasons for which we joined. The
affinity groups we have within the fraternity are evidence of this. RV clubs,
equestrian groups, clowns, charitable outreach, public school support efforts,
degree teams, and many others are examples of the variety of interests of our
members.
One distinction we have in Freemasonry is our ritual. I believe that
many of us revel in the beauty of a well-delivered degree. And we cringe at the
sights and sounds of poorly prepared work. Some of our Brothers can deliver the
lectures at a level of quality that Sir Lawrence Olivier would have stood and
applauded. And others love to listen to the beautiful meaning conveyed in such
elegant work. Ritual is something that makes us different and provides
enjoyment.
Yet in each group, the effectiveness and longevity is determined by the
quality of the experience, and so long as they have men interested in spending
time with the other men in that endeavor.
If we spent half as much effort in identifying the interests of our current
members, as we do trying to attract new members, we would begin to understand
why we joined. If we could improve on the quality of the experiences we offer to
the craft, we would be better prepared to welcome new members.
If we want a ten percent increase in active membership, then don’t go to
the streets. Go to the phones and call your Brethren. Find out what they are
interested in. Don’t ask them why they no longer attend. We already know the
answer. Find out how we can do better in the areas that interest them.
If they have new interests, then find out what they want to do. If they
want to watch sporting events, then put a widescreen TV in the Lodge, a satellite
dish on the roof, and pick up your Brothers on your way to the Lodge and have
them open a bag of chips.
If they want to help improve the quality of education in your local
school district, then pick them up on your way to a meeting with the principal of
the school closest to your Lodge and help them to become involved again.
Every Lodge will be as different as the members who comprise its
membership rolls. Your job, my job, is to reach out to those with whom we want
to spend time and find out what is meaningful to them.
Our inactive membership doesn’t need to be replaced, they need to be
engaged.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

199

Inability to cover expenses is not the best reason to grow the
membership of an organization. I refuse to believe that Freemasonry is no longer
relevant in our society. I believe the way we practice Freemasonry may no longer
be relevant in our society.
If our fraternity does not change the ways in which we deliver
Freemasonry to our membership, how can we expect to see any difference in the
participation rates?
If the vast majority of our fraternity is no longer interested in what we
do, then how can we expect new members to stay engaged?
If we do not focus our attentions to our current members, then how can
we expect them to perceive greater value in the fraternity.
I believe our Brethren in Freemasonry are good men and entitled to receive our
primary attentions. If you believe our primary purpose is to recruit new members
and to confer degrees, then start a school and charge tuition.
I believe our primary purpose is brotherly love, relief and truth. I
believe we should be a place where men can spend time together in whatever
activities bring them enjoyment.
I don’t need more Brothers, I need more fulfilled friends who are
Brothers. We don’t need to increase our membership numbers for numbers sake,
but we do need to increase our membership participation. The members of this
fraternity need to remember why they joined, and this fraternity needs to
improve on the delivery of that commitment before anything else.
I am always on the lookout for friends who can become brothers. I
simply believe that we should spend more time looking for brothers who can
become our friends.
Thank you my Brethren, and Most Worshipful for this opportunity.
Respectfully submitted,
Richard K. Pumerantz, Grand Orator

nd

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Grand Master Whitaker’s Decision No. 2
Use of Refreshment Revolving Fund
I have been asked to determine the intent of Section 21020 of the
California Masonic Code, which provides for two types of revolving funds. The
first type is for the use of the Lodge Secretary, and the second type is for use by
the Stewards or Junior Warden, as the Master may determine, “in providing
refreshments for all regular Lodge functions.” It is usually called the
Refreshment Revolving Fund.
Some Lodges have used this “Refreshment Revolving Fund” for
purposes other than the provision of simple “refreshments” before or after
meetings. They have used it for dinners, and even for the operation of other
social activities of the Lodge. That was never the intent of this section of the
code, and I have issued my Decision No. 2 to clarify the real intention of Section
21020.
When Section 21020 is read along with Section 21090, it is obvious
that “refreshments” were intended to be supplied by the Lodge, and not
something for which the Lodge is reimbursed by those participating in the
“refreshments.” The code does have a provision for the handling of dinners
where tickets are sold and the Lodge is reimbursed for funds which may have
been advanced for the provision of meals. That is Section 21060 of the code,
which provides for appropriate segregation of such funds when “collected by an
officer, committee, or member of a Lodge from members of the Lodge for social
purposes of the Lodge.” Section 21020 is not intended as a fund to be used for a
dinner where a charge is made to offset or defray the cost of the meal. Section
21060 is the proper section of the code governing the latter.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretive decisions are recorded:
Section 21020 pertaining to the use of the Refreshment Revolving Fund
is intended to provide complimentary refreshments for Lodge members and their
guests, with no charge being made for these refreshments. The fund is a
“revolving fund” in that once the fund is exhausted the Lodge may appropriate
an amount not-to-exceed $500 at any one time. This section of the Code also
requires the presentation of receipts for these incidental expenses to the Lodge
by the Stewards or Junior Warden, as directed by the Master, in order for

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

205

another appropriation to be made. There is no other authorized use of this fund,
and its use for any other purpose is prohibited by the California Masonic Code.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
In Decision No. 2, the Grand Master determined the proper use of the
Refreshment Revolving Fund. Some Lodges have used this fund for purposes
other than provision of simple refreshments before or after a Lodge Meeting or
activity.
Refreshment is defined in the dictionary as: things that refresh; food or
drink; light meal.
The California Masonic Code in Section 21020 provides for the
establishment of a refreshment fund and in Section 21090 states the maximum
amount that may be spent for refreshments. It is obvious that refreshments were
intended to be supplied by the Lodge and not to be reimbursed by those
participating in the refreshments.
Section 21060 of the Code has provisions for handling of funds for the
payment of the necessary discretionary and social expenses of the Lodge. These
funds can only be used for the intended purpose for which they were collected.
This section of the Code covers the provision for the handling of dinners and
other activities where funds are collected and the Lodge is reimbursed for
expenses that may have been incurred concerning these functions.
Grand Master’s Decision No. 2 is consistent with the policy and general
purposes of Grand Lodge.
Decision AFFIRMED.
Grand Master Whitaker’s Decision No. 3
Use of Websites
In 1993 Grand Lodge approved Grand Master Doan’s Decision No. 5,
and in 1996 Grand Lodge approved Grand Master Alexander’s Decision No. 3
(and its companion, Recommendation No. 1), which interpreted Section 27600
and 27700 of the California Masonic Code. Those sections of the code pertain
to advertising in trestleboards by Lodges, and by concordant and appendant
bodies. Since those decisions were approved by Grand Lodge, many Lodges
have turned to the Internet as an extension of their Lodge trestleboard. I have
been asked as Grand Master whether Lodge websites are subject to the
provisions of Section 27600 of the California Masonic Code, and I have ruled
that they are. If that is so, then if advertising is placed on the Lodge website it
must be available to members of the Lodge only (as would be the case with the
printed trestleboard), and subject to the other rules in Section 27600.

206

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

By this ruling a Lodge is not prohibited from making its website
available to the general public. In fact most Lodge websites are established so
that the general public is aware of its activities, and so that sojourning Masons
can find the Lodge and visit it. Moreover, Grand Lodge provides a free website
service to any Lodges who wish to use it through the Institute for Masonic
Studies. However, I have ruled that no public website may have advertising on it
since this would be a violation of Section 27600 of the code. A Lodge may
include commercial advertising on a website that is restricted to its own
members only, but may not do so when the site is open to non-members. By
extension, a Lodge may not use a website hosted by a commercial business
which advertises businesses not owned by members of the Lodge.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretive decisions are recorded:
A Lodge may make its website available to the general public.
However, the website may not be hosted by a provider which inserts banner,
pop-up or other advertising on the Lodge’s site. Commercial advertising may
appear on a Lodge’s website only if (1) access to the website is limited to Lodge
members, (2) the advertising is from members and their business or commercial
establishments and (3) all other requirements of Section 27600 are met.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Our Grand Master has decided that Lodge websites are subject to the
provisions of Section 27600 of the California Masonic Code.
Section 27600 states in part: “ Advertising may be included in
publications which the Lodge uses to communicate with its members…”, and
continues with several terms and conditions.
Grand Master Alexander’s Decision No. 3 and Recommendation No. 1
states: “ A Masonic Lodge may permit advertising only in Lodge publications.
This includes any publication which the Lodge uses to communicate with its
members.”
Grand Master Nahin stated in his letter to all Lodge Masters in March
of 1999: “Lodge websites or home pages are Lodge publications.”
The Grand Lodge policy of maintaining good taste and certain bounds
of propriety relating to Lodge publications has been consistent and unchanging
for many years. To extend this policy to websites is appropriate and consistent
with Grand Master Nahin’s directive.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

207

This Decision is consistent with Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.
Decision AFFIRMED
Grand Master Whitaker’s Decision No. 4
Solicitation of Funds to Participate in Foundation Outside of California
Section 21010 of the California Masonic Code states that “A Lodge
shall not establish or form a foundation separate from the Lodge with power to
receive, hold, administer, invest or reinvest funds received by the Lodge.” I
have been asked if a Lodge in this jurisdiction can “join” a foundation in another
jurisdiction for the purpose of soliciting “tax-exempt” funds for community
service projects that the Lodge wishes to support. After carefully reviewing the
language of this section, I have ruled that a Lodge cannot do so because it would
circumvent the clear intention of Section 21010.
If a Lodge solicits contributions from its community it must, by
definition, “receive” those funds. If it redirects them to a foundation in another
state, it is “investing” those funds in order to make them “tax exempt” for the
donors. While a Lodge cannot forbid its members to contribute to a foundation
in another state, the Lodge cannot “join” with such a foundation and participate
through a “membership fee” in order to “pass through” funds solicited by the
Lodge for such a foundation. In addition, Sections 27600 and 27800 set forth
the conditions under which a Lodge may solicit contributions from the public.
Membership in a “foundation” in another jurisdiction does not qualify under
these sections.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretive decisions are recorded:
Lodges in our jurisdiction may not hold memberships in programs
offered by tax-exempt foundations in other jurisdictions in order to raise funds
for community projects.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The California Masonic Code prohibits Lodges from establishing
foundations on their own. The Grand Master was asked if a Lodge could join an
already established foundation, one that created a loophole whereby Masons
could obtain tax credits for donations to community service projects. In his
Decision No. 4, the Grand Master states that the Code prohibits such an action.
In this particular circumstance our law may appear to some to be
unreasonably restrictive. However, as will be discussed in other legislation as

208

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

well, it must be appreciated that tax laws are very complicated. Lodges cannot
be put in the position of making guarantees to its members that one or another
plan is legitimate. In so doing, an individual Lodge could be held responsible
for subsequent tax liabilities of any member who then makes a donation in good
faith. To avoid such problems, our law was purposely made restrictive. There is
no prohibition preventing individual Masons from making donations to any
charitable cause they wish. However, it is better for individual Lodges to stay
out of the charity business. This Decision is consistent with our current Grand
Lodge policy.
Decision AFFIRMED.
Grand Master Whitaker’s Decision No. 5
Investments of Grand Lodge and Its Entities
The 1975 Edition of the California Masonic Code limited the amount of
Grand Lodge funds that could be invested in common stock. In 1988, then
Grand Master Leo B. Mark, believing the applicable code section to be
somewhat ambiguous, decided that funds may not be joined or “pooled” with
other funds to comply with the common stock limitation. The goal of the
Decision was obviously to prevent the circumvention of the limitation on the
amount that could be invested in common stock.
In 1991, when the code was revised, the Code Revision Committee
incorporated Grand Master Mark’s interpretive Decision directly into the code,
making it law. However, in so doing, the Committee, through inadvertence,
added to Section 7020 the following language: “…no funds of any entity may
be pooled with funds of any other entity.”
This language, although intended to deal only with the common stock
issue, was stated so broadly as to appear to preclude any pooling – something
which had not been the prior law, and something never envisioned in Grand
Master Mark’s Decision. The Code Revision Commission was not authorized to
make new law. It was charged with clarifying the 1975 Edition of the California
Masonic Code. In this case the result was the opposite. Rather than clarifying
an issue pertaining to the amount that could be invested in common stock it had
the effect of eliminating the use of mutual funds, and other such ordinary
financial practices.
The investment activities of the Grand Lodge, the Masonic Homes of
California, the California Masonic Foundation and others can greatly benefit by
the combining of investment funds. In fact, this seemed so obvious that Grand
Lodge and its entities have in fact been investing in mutual funds for at least six
year. It was only a close reading of this section of the code by the Investment
Committee that called into question the language that is presently in Section

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

209

7020 of the 1991 Edition of the California Masonic Code. Careful research into
the history of this section by the Jurisprudence Committee revealed the error as
outlined above. It was never the intention of the Code Revision Committee to
change the 1975 code; but in this case, that may well have happened. It is my
decision, therefore that the intent of the 1975 code should be followed when
Section 7020 is read, and that pooling of assets for investment purposes is
authorized. Moreover, in order to make sure that the true intention of this
section is clear for the future, I have also introduced a Grand Master’s
Recommendation to restore the language to the 1975 intent. While unnecessary
to amend the law as well, it is my belief that we should take care of it now so that
this question does not arise again.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretive decisions are recorded:
The intent of the 1975 code should be followed when Section 7020 of
the 1991 code is read: pooling of assets belonging to two or more Grand Lodge
entities for investment purposes is authorized.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master’s Decision No. 5 keeps in effect current Grand Lodge
investment practices under California Masonic Code Section 7020. This current
investment policy, in use for at least the past six years, permits Grand Lodge to
place money from different entities, such as the Masonic Homes Endowment
Fund and California Masonic Memorial Temple, in the same investment vehicle,
for example a common stock mutual fund. It is both convenient and practical to
do this, and past experience has shown this practice to be beneficial. The
amounts invested and the return on investment for each entity is strictly and
separately accounted for. Each entity receives investment income in exact
proportion to the amount invested by that entity. The present language of
Section 7020 is ambiguous; this Decision makes clear that current Grand Lodge
investment practices are appropriate and consistent with that Section. This
Decision is also the basis for Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 3, which will
add language to Section 7020 to remove any ambiguity.
This Decision is consistent with Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.
Decision AFFIRMED.

Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 1
Safeguards for Lodge Finances and Investments
The Grand Lodge Investment Committee has asked me to propose
changes in the California Masonic Code to better safeguard the finances and
investments of our Lodges. I am therefore asking you to adopt the changes
below in our law to better protect Lodge assets.
If you adopt this recommendation, the California Masonic Code will be
amended as marked:
§21010. CONTROL AND USE.
A Lodge must retain control of its own funds and property (other than
real property). It may deposit its funds only in a sound and reputable
depository institution bank or banks that is regulated and insured
(including FDIC for banks, NCUA for credit unions or SIPC for
brokerages). A Chartered Lodge may, after receiving consent from the
appropriate Grand Lodge Committee on Temple Properties and
Taxation, cause the incorporation of a separate entity Temple
Association for the purpose of purchasing, erecting or maintaining and
holding title to real estate for a temple for Lodge uses and purposes;
and a Lodge or Lodges may hold shares of stock in such corporation.
Space in sSuch temple real estate may be rented for commercial and
other uses consistent with the purposes of the Lodge or the corporation
and not in violation of this Code.
A Lodge shall not incorporate. It shall not divide its funds or property
with its members, even those withdrawing to form a new Lodge or with
the new Lodge thus formed. A Lodge shall not divide its funds with an
existing Lodge by way of a contribution.
A Lodge shall adopt a budget for the following twelve months at the
first Stated Meeting held after the annual installation of the officers of
the Lodge.
No money shall be drawn without the express consent of the Lodge,
even though included in a Lodge budget, except that:
A.

Dispensed by the Charity Committee;

B.

Due Grand Lodge; and

C.

Required to pay the taxes or other charges of local, State
or Federal governmental agencies.

Lodge may approve bills against the Lodge only when the bills are
supported by proper documentation, such as an invoice or receipt. No

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

warrant check or draft shall be drawn issued unless there are sufficient
funds to honor it.
A Lodge shall not invest any of its funds in any manner or obligate
itself in any way for the purpose of acquiring land or building a
Masonic temple except upon compliance with the provisions of this
Code.
A Lodge shall not establish or form a foundation separate from the
Lodge with power to receive, hold, administer, invest or reinvest funds
received by the Lodge.
§21030. INVESTMENT.
Exclusive of a Lodge’s investment in an incorporated Temple
Association real estate entity as set forth in Section 21010, a Lodge may
invest its funds only as provided in this section. All Lodge investments
shall be held in the name of the Lodge. The Lodge’s investments
allowed under this section shall be administered either by an investment
committee appointed by the Master or by a Board of Trustees elected
by the Lodge. If a Lodge uses a Board of Trustees elected by the
Lodge, tThe Trustees shall be governed by a standing resolution of the
Lodge, the form of which shall be promulgated by the Grand Master.
The Trustees shall consist of at least three members of the Lodge, each
serving staggered three-year terms, and shall be elected at the Stated
Meeting next succeeding the annual installation of Lodge officers.
Each Trustee shall sign, and be governed by, a Conflict-of-Interest
Statement as promulgated by the Grand Master. One who acts as
trustee, for other than the Lodge, under any trust agreement by the
terms of which the Lodge has a present or future interest, direct or
indirect, is ineligible as a Lodge trustee, and the Master shall
immediately remove him as a Lodge trustee when the Master becomes
aware of such other position. The vacancy shall be filled in the manner
provided for in the standing resolution.
The Lodge Trustees shall have the power to acquire, sell and convey all
Lodge investments and to invest and reinvest its funds. The Trustees
may exercise such powers, provided that the Trustees shall always be
under the control of the Lodge, and may not make any final investment
decisions without prior approval of the Lodge. The Trustees may take
emergency action without prior Lodge approval only if they deem the
action to be financially necessary and prudent. In such circumstances,
the action taken will be presented to the Lodge at the next Stated
Meeting.

The Lodge Trustees shall provide a full oral and written report of their
activities as such at the February and August Lodge Stated Meetings.

the investments since the previous report.
Through the Lodge investment committee or Board of Trustees, the
Lodge shall confer and counsel as to any proposed or contemplated

A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than fair market value of the
property. Where there is no ready market to establish the fair market

and independent appraiser.

§21040. TITLE TO ASSETS.
A Lodge may hold title to all of its assets with the power to acquire,
mortgage, sell and convey property and to invest and reinvest its funds.
It may, through trustees provided for in a standing resolution, exercise
such powers, provided that they shall always be under the control of the
Lodge, and may not buy property for the Lodge or sell property of the
Lodge without prior approval of the Lodge.
One who acts as trustee, for other than the Lodge, under any trust
agreement by the terms of which the Lodge has a present or future
interest, direct or indirect, is ineligible as a Lodge trustee and the
Master shall immediately remove him. The office shall be filled in the
manner provided for in the standing resolution.
A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than fair market value of the
property.
§21050. LIFE MEMBERSHIP.
The original amounts paid for life memberships shall be forever kept
and maintained as and for a permanent Life Membership Fund. Only
interest or dividends paid in cash on bank deposits or authorized
investments in securities investment earnings and realized capital gains
of the Fund shall be available for general Lodge use. A Lodge may
invest its life membership funds provided for in this Code under Section
21030 B if such investments are kept separate and apart from all other
Lodge assets.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Our Grand Master, at the request of the Grand Lodge Investment
Committee, recommends amendments to California Masonic Code Sections
21010, 21030 and 21050 with deletion of 21040, all of which deal with Lodge
funds and real estate.
Presently a Lodge has the option to provide for the corporate holding of
real estate. Adoption of this Recommendation, including the revision from the
optional or permissive word “may” to the mandatory word “shall” would require
that all real estate of Lodges be held and administered by a corporation.
Such incorporation can provide a Lodge, its members and its funds a
shield from liability for personal injuries and financial risks due to loan
foreclosures. As Lodges cannot incorporate pursuant to Section 21010,
corporate protection would be afforded through incorporated ownership.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

215

This Recommendation would also permit a standardized procedure for
ownership of real property throughout our Lodges in California with Uniform
Articles of Incorporation and Uniform By-Laws already established by Grand
Lodge pursuant to Section 22010.
The explanation of the technical provisions regarding conflicts of
interest, reports, limitation of investment of funds and administration of
investments by a Board of Trustees elected by the Lodge are referred to in the
Grand Master’s Recommendation and need not be reiterated.
Each of the recommendations further safeguard Lodge finances and
investments and therefore this Recommendation serves the best interests and
general purposes of our Grand Lodge.
Recommendation CARRIED-OVER.

Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 2
Restructuring of Grand Lodge Committees
During recent years, and in particular the one about to conclude, Grand
Masters and their respective Executive Committees have attempted to improve
the communication between themselves, and the various boards and committees
which perform much of the work of Grand Lodge. Members of the Executive
Committee serve as ex-officio members of the various boards and have also been
assigned by the Grand Master to serve as a liaison to the several committees.
This was designed to give better overall direction to their work, and also to help
the Grand Master better understand their problems and needs.
All this has resulted in greater expectation from our committees at the
same time that financial resources to fund them has decreased. It therefore
seems appropriate to recommend a plan of reorganization for our Grand Lodge
committees which will avoid duplication, and improve the results at less cost.
I therefore propose that the reorganization of our present standing
committee structure outlined below be approved effective with the close of the
2003 Annual Communication:

§2040. GRAND SECRETARY.
It shall be the duty of the Grand Secretary to:
A. Staff and manage the office of Grand Lodge, establish and
administer all personnel policies, prepare job descriptions and
define the duties of office employees, supervise all Grand Lodge
personnel whose offices are within the Grand Lodge office,
evaluate those employees’ performance on a regular basis;
B. Supervise, subject to the ultimate control of the Trustees of the
Masonic Homes of California, the Trustees of the California
Masonic Memorial Temple, the Trustees of the California Masonic
Foundation, the Grand Treasurer, or the governing board of any
other entity of Grand Lodge, as the case may be, all clerical,
administrative or accounting personnel employed by or assigned to
such officer or entity, whose offices are within the Grand Lodge
office;
C. Serve as Secretary of record for various Boards and other entities
of Grand Lodge as provided for elsewhere in this Code, but these
duties shall not require that he be a member of such Boards and
entities nor that he personally attend each meeting to record the
minutes thereof. He shall maintain files of all minutes of such
Boards and entities, taken by him or his designee, in a safe place in
the Grand Lodge office;
D. Record all transactions of Grand Lodge proper to be written;
oversee the publication of its Proceedings, Roster and Masonic
Code; forward, without charge, one copy of the annual Proceedings
and Roster to each Lodge, and one copy of the annual Proceedings,
Roster and Masonic Code, or amendments thereto, to those
designated by the Grand Master;
E. Provide statistical information for inclusion in the Proceedings of
Grand Lodge;
F.

Receive, duly file and safely keep, in a fire-resistive storage
facility, when necessary, all essential papers and documents
addressed or belonging to Grand Lodge;

G. Conduct the correspondence of Grand Lodge and communicate
with other Grand Lodges on behalf of the Constituent Lodges and
members of this Jurisdiction;
H. Keep the Seals of Grand Lodge and its entities and affix the
appropriate seal, with his attestation, to all instruments emanating
from Grand Lodge and its entities when required;

§3040. GRAND CHAPLAIN.
The Grand Chaplain shall present a devotional service at each Annual
Communication of Grand Lodge. During the Annual Communication,
the Grand Chaplain shall also present a fitting memorial service for
members of Grand Lodge, or other distinguished Master Masons of this
Jurisdiction, who were called from their earthly toil during the
preceding year. He and shall also perform such other services,
appertaining to his office, as may be required of him by the Grand
Master.
§6020. STANDING.
The members of standing committees shall be appointed by the newly
installed Grand Master at each Annual Communication before the close
thereof.
The following are standing committees:

§6050. TEMPLE MASONIC PROPERTIES AND TAXATION.
The Committee on Temple Masonic Properties shall examine and
approve all details regarding any contract involving a Lodge, Temple
Association, committee, partnership or corporation or other entity
representing itself to be Masonic in character, or operating for the
benefit of a Lodge or Lodges in this Jurisdiction for:
A.

Any option or contract for the purchase of real property, the
encumbrance of real property or the lease of real property, as
lessor or lessee, for more than three years;

B.

A contract for or commencement of the construction,
alteration, improvement, replacement, repair, or maintenance
of a Masonic Temple in excess of $25,000; and

C.

Any option or contract regarding the sale or listing for sale of
any real property owned by such entity.

The committee shall, subject to the approval of the Grand Master,
establish rules and regulations governing the processing of applications
for committee approval.
The committee shall review the reports of transactions of Temple
Associations submitted to the Grand Secretary as provided for

221

222

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

elsewhere in this Code and may give counsel and advise concerning the
operation of those Temple Associations.
The committee shall, also, advise Lodges and the Grand Lodge on
matters of taxation with respect to income, property, sales and
employment taxes.
§6060. PUBLIC SCHOOLS.
The Committee on Public Schools shall develop and promote methods
of increasing interest in and support for the public schools.
§6060. LODGE SUPPORT.
The Committee on Lodge Support shall develop program materials for
use by Lodges in their activities in support of the public schools and the
observance of the United States Constitution, the Grand Master’s
Achievement Award and such other Lodge programs as may from time
to time be approved by the Grand Master. These materials shall serve
as a resource for Lodge programs and shall be published in electronic
format as well as traditional written format for dissemination to the
Lodges.
§6065.

MASONIC HOMES ENDOWMENT FRATERNAL
SUPPORT SERVICES.

The Committee on Masonic Homes Endowment Fraternal Support
Services shall develop and implement strategies designed to promote
annual contributions to the Masonic Homes General and Endowment
Funds. The Committee shall:
A.

Provide communications, through presentations and written
materials, to Lodges and other interested parties, to increase
awareness and encourage and promote annual contributions;

B.

Respond to and initiate contact with California Masons who
may be interested in providing major financial support to the
Homes and refer them to Grand Lodge and Homes
representatives for further cultivation; and

C.

Present a year-end contribution report at the Grand Lodge
Annual Communication.
The Committee shall also develop and promote methods of
interest in and support for Masonic widows.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

§6070. UNITED STATES CONSTITUTION OBSERVANCE.
The Committee on United States Constitution Observance shall develop
and promote methods of interest in and support for the Constitution of
the United States and other patriotic observances.
§6080. CHARTERS.
The Committee on Charters shall:
A.

Conduct an investigation, including examination, of the
records and proceedings of organizations seeking Lodge
status, and report its findings and recommendations to the
Grand Master; and

B.

Counsel with Lodges under dispensation and examine their
records and proceedings and report its findings and
recommendations to the Grand Lodge prior to the close of the
next Annual Communication.

§6090.

GRAND LODGE WEEK ACTIVITIES ANNUAL
COMMUNICATION ACTIVITIES.

The Committee on Grand Lodge Week Activities Annual
Communication Activities shall register and report the number
registered to Grand Lodge of members, elected representatives and
guests of the Grand Lodge, and other Master Masons, and validate their
credentials. It shall report to Grand Lodge the Lodges which are not
represented. In addition, the Committee shall provide such other
programs and staff for the conduct of the Annual Communication, as
the Grand Master shall direct shall attend to entertainment during Grand
Lodge Week as directed by the Grand Master.
§6100. FINANCE.
The Committee on Finance shall:
A.

Assist and consult with the Grand Master, Grand Treasurer
and Grand Secretary regarding the financial affairs of Grand
Lodge;

B.

Advise and assist all boards in financial matters, when
requested;

C.

Assist in the preparation of the Grand Lodge Budget to the

223

224

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

extent provided in Section 7040 of this Code;
D.

Estimate and make provision for the reimbursement of the
expenses of Grand Lodge officers, Past Grand Masters,
Inspectors, and members of committees and boards as
approved by the Grand Master or adopted by Grand Lodge;

E.

Supervise compliance with the adopted budget of Grand
Lodge;

F.

Prescribe procedures for the filing of all expense claims and be
responsible for approval or disallowance of the claims;

G.

Report on every appropriation of money before it can be made;

H.

Serve as the Retirement Plan Committee of the California
Masonic Retirement Plan when appointed as such pursuant to
such plan; and

H.I.

Upon receipt from the Grand Secretary of proposed
resolutions and recommendations to be considered at the next
Annual Communication, review them and prepare an estimate
of the cost impact of each, if adopted, and transmit, at least 75
days prior to the commencement date of the next Annual
Communication, a report of such cost estimates to the Grand
Secretary.

Every over-expenditure of budgeted items, needing supplemental
appropriations approved by the Grand Master, shall be referred to and
reported on by this Committee.
No appropriation of money shall be made at any Annual
Communication after the election of Grand Officers, except by a
three-fourths vote of the members present.
§6120. CREDENTIALS.
The Committee on Credentials shall register and report the number
registered to Grand Lodge of members, elected representatives and
guests of the Grand Lodge, and other Master Masons, and validate their
credentials. It shall report to Grand Lodge the Lodges which are not
represented.
§6140. MASONIC HISTORY.
The Committee on Masonic History shall gather, preserve and
document historic facts relating to Grand Lodge and its constituent
Lodges.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

§6150. MEMORIAL SERVICE.
The Committee on Memorial Service, during the Annual
Communication, shall present a fitting memorial service for members of
Grand Lodge, or other distinguished Master Masons of this Jurisdiction,
who were called from their earthly toil during the preceding year.
§6160. MASONIC EDUCATION.
The Committee on Masonic Education shall assist Grand Lodge and
constituent Lodges in promoting Masonic education. Subject to the
approval of the Grand Master, it may regulate the publication and
dissemination in this Jurisdiction of materials emanating from research
Lodges, research clubs, research organizations, or research committees
of this or any other Jurisdiction.
§6200. MASONIC SERVICE BUREAUS.
The Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus shall supervise the
activities of Masonic Service Bureaus and assist them to standardize
their operations. It may formulate rules and amendments necessary for
their effective operation, subject to the approval of Grand Lodge.
It shall process all applications for the formation of new bureaus and
shall submit them, with its recommendations, to Grand Lodge or the
Grand Master.
All applications from Masonic Service Bureaus or Lodges for funds or
reimbursement shall be submitted to this committee at least 60 days
prior to each Annual Communication of Grand Lodge. The Committee
shall make a thorough investigation of the merits of each application
and report its findings and recommendations to the Committee on
Finance on or before the opening day of the Annual Communication.
If, in the opinion of this Committee, good cause is shown that
immediate reimbursement is essential, reimbursement may be made
with the approval of the Grand Master.
§21010. CONTROL AND USE.
A Lodge must retain control of its own funds and property. It may
deposit its funds in a sound and reputable bank or banks. A Chartered
Lodge may, after receiving consent from the Grand Lodge Committee
on Temple Masonic Properties and Taxation, cause the incorporation of

225

226

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

a Temple Association for the purpose of purchasing, erecting or
maintaining and holding title to real estate for a temple for Lodge uses
and purposes, and a Lodge or Lodges may hold shares of stock in such
corporation. Space in such temple may be rented for commercial and
other uses consistent with the purposes of the Lodge or the corporation
and not in violation of this Code.
A Lodge shall not incorporate. It shall not divide its funds or property
with its members, even those withdrawing to form a new Lodge or with
the new Lodge thus formed. A Lodge shall not divide its funds with an
existing Lodge by way of a contribution.
A Lodge shall adopt a budget for the following twelve months at the
first Stated Meeting held after the annual installation of the officers of
the Lodge.
No money shall be drawn without the express consent of the Lodge,
even though included in a Lodge budget, except that:
A.

Dispensed by the Charity Committee;

B.

Due Grand Lodge; and

C.

Required to pay the taxes or other charges of local, State or
Federal governmental agencies.

Lodges may approve bills against the Lodge only when the bills are
supported by proper documentation, such as an invoice or receipt. No
warrant shall be drawn unless there are sufficient funds to honor it.
A Lodge shall not invest any of its funds in any manner or obligate
itself in any way for the purpose of acquiring land or building a
Masonic temple except upon compliance with the provisions of this
Code.
A Lodge shall not establish or form a foundation separate from the
Lodge with power to receive, hold, administer, invest or reinvest funds
received by the Lodge.
§22010. ORGANIZATION AND OPERATION.
All Temple Associations shall be organized and shall operate as
follows:
A.

A Temple Association may be organized either as a non-profit
or as a general law corporation under the laws of the State in
which it owns or is to acquire real estate, land or a building;

B.

The exclusive purpose for which a Temple Association may be
organized is to hold title to property, collect income therefrom

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

and turn over the entire amount thereof, less expenses, to its
members or shareholders, all of which must be organizations
described in Section 501(c) of the Internal Revenue Code of
1954. A Temple Association shall not engage in any activities
or exercise any powers that are not in furtherance of this
exclusive purpose. No part of the net earnings of a Temple
Association shall inure to the benefit of any member or private
shareholder, as defined for purposes of Section 501(c)(7) of
the Internal Revenue Code of 1954;
C.

Uniform Articles of Incorporation and By-Laws for Temple
Associations written by the Grand Lodge Committee on
Temple Masonic Properties, with the approval of the Grand
Master, shall be adopted for use by each Temple Association.
Any amendments to such Uniform Articles or By-laws by the
Grand Lodge Committee on Temple Masonic Properties, with
the approval of the Grand Master, also shall be adopted for use
by each Temple Association. Appropriate revisions to the
Uniform Articles and By-laws may be authorized by the
Committee on Temple Masonic Properties for Temple
Associations with more than one member or shareholder;

D.

A Lodge shall exercise its rights as a member or shareholder of
a Temple Association, including its right to elect or remove
directors, pursuant only to the authorization of a majority of
members of the Lodge present at a Stated Meeting of the
Lodge at which a quorum is present. Such authorization shall
not be contested if written instruments, executed by the Master
or Secretary of the Lodge, setting forth the authorization are
filed with the Secretary of the Temple Association. The Lodge
may exercise its rights through the Master of the Lodge, or any
person authorized by proxy or power of attorney duly executed
by him who is authorized to execute any documents and to
vote the Lodge’s membership or shares at any members’ or
shareholders’ meeting of the Temple Association in
accordance with the terms of any action previously authorized
by the Lodge;

E.

A Temple Association shall submit a written report to each
Lodge which is a member or shareholder of the Association on
August 1 of each year and on the following February 1. The
report shall be presented at the next Stated Meeting of the
Lodge. The August 1 report shall include a statement of
income and expenditures for the six month period ending on
the preceding June 30, together with a statement of the assets
and liabilities of the Association on the preceding June 30.

227

228

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The February 1 report shall include a statement of income and
expenditures for the twelve month period ending on the
preceding December 31, together with a statement of the assets
and liabilities of the Association on the preceding December
31. A copy of each such report shall be given to the Inspector
of the District to which such Lodge is assigned;
F.

Annually on or before the 15th day of the fifth month
following the end of its fiscal year, each Temple Association
shall submit to the Grand Secretary a full and correct report of
its transactions for that preceding fiscal year on a form as may
be provided. The report shall include a statement of assets,
liabilities and net worth as of the end of the 12 month period.
Temple Associations formed during the 12 month period shall
transmit a report from the date of incorporation;

G.

A Temple Association may not hold or administer funds for
any purpose not specifically set forth in this Code; and

H.

Temple Associations funds, not Lodge funds, must be used to
compensate employees.

§22020. INVESTMENT IN TEMPLE ASSOCIATIONS.
A Lodge, after first obtaining the consent of the Grand Lodge
Committee on Temple Masonic Properties, may also invest its funds in
or maintain an investment in:
A.

B.

Memberships or shares in a Temple Association wherein a
Masonic Lodge or Masonic Lodges collectively hold a
majority of memberships or shares or when a controlling
interest is acquired by such investment.
The Temple
Association shall be one that:
1.

Owns real estate embracing a building suitable for the
occupancy in whole or in part by a Masonic Lodge;

2.

Owns land upon which it is proposed to erect such a
building;

3.

Is formed for the purpose of acquiring such real estate
or building; or

4.

Owns such other investments in real property which
have received the specific approval of the Grand
Lodge Committee on Temple Masonic Properties and
Taxation.

A loan evidenced by:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

1.

A duly executed promissory note amply secured by a
first lien against property of a class described under
paragraphs A.1 or A.2; or

2.

A promissory note duly executed by a Temple
Association in which the Lodge owns a controlling
interest.

§25140. DUTIES OF MASTER.
It shall be the duty of the Master to:
A.

Preside at all meetings of the Lodge;

B.

Confer or have conferred all degrees in strict accordance with
the ritual which has been, or may hereafter be, approved by
Grand Lodge;

C.

Give, or have given, in full, the lectures appertaining to each
degree, at the time it is conferred, in accordance with the
ritual;

D.

Appoint those officers as provided for in the By-laws;

E.

Appoint an Auditing Committee and a Committee on
Delinquent Members at the Stated Meeting next succeeding his
installation;

F.

Appoint a chairman to coordinate with the Grand Lodge
Committees on Lodge Support Public Schools, United States
Constitution Observance, Masonic Education and Masonic
Homes Endowment Fraternal Support Services and any Grand
Lodge program charged with Masonic education;

G.

Attest at each Stated Meeting of his Lodge all minutes or
records of proceedings as approved at such meetings by the
Lodge;

H.

Superintend the official acts of all the officers of his Lodge
and to see that their respective duties are properly discharged;

I.

Carefully guard against any infraction, by the members of his
Lodge, of its own By-laws, of the Constitution or Ordinances
of Grand Lodge, or of the General Regulations of Masonry;
and

J.

Provide for the installation of his successor.

§25230. DUTIES OF SECRETARY.

229

230

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

It shall be the duty of the Secretary to:
A.

Record all proceedings at each meeting proper to be written,
under the direction of the Master, and to transcribe the same in
a minute book to be kept for that purpose, and at the next
Stated Meeting to read the minutes to the Lodge and, after they
are approved, to present them to the Master for his signature.
After approval no change may be made except by appropriate
motion properly recorded;

B.

Present to the Lodge at each August Stated Meeting, a
statement of income and expenditures, assets and liabilities, on
a form promulgated by the Grand Master, for the six months
ended on the preceding June 30, and at each February Stated
Meeting a similar statement for the 12 months ended on the
preceding December 31, and to submit a copy thereof to the
Inspector of the District to which the Lodge is assigned and to
the Grand Master;

C.

Prepare and transmit a copy of such record, or of any part
thereof, to Grand Lodge, when required;

D.

Collect and receive all monies due the Lodge and pay them to
the Treasurer;

§28020. MASONIC SERVICE BUREAUS.
Masonic Service Bureaus shall be for the purpose of:
A.

Properly relieving the distress of Masons who are members of
Lodges in other areas of this Jurisdiction or of Lodges in other
Jurisdictions, their widows and orphans, while residing or
visiting in the area of the Masonic Service Bureau;

B.

Arranging and conducting funerals of such deceased Masons;

C.

Making investigations requested by Lodges of this or other
Jurisdictions;

D.

Visiting sick sojourning Masons;

E.

Operating a clearing house as to applicants for the degrees and
for dual or plural membership by affiliation of Lodges in the
area of that Masonic Service Bureau; and

F.

Such Masonic activities as may be authorized by the
Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus as provided in this
Code subject to appeal and review of the Grand Master or
Grand Lodge.

Lodges may form a Masonic Service Bureau under a compact to be
agreed upon among themselves and approved by the Grand Lodge or
Grand Master before becoming effective. They may provide, among
other things, for paying the expenses of the work in such manner as they
shall deem proper.
The Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus shall, with the consent and
by direction of the Grand Master, have the power to compel any Lodge
within this Grand Jurisdiction to become a member of the Masonic
Service Bureau nearest its place of meeting. The Committee on
Masonic Service Bureaus may, when the interests of the Craft will be
served, cause to be organized with the consent of and by direction of
the Grand Master additional Masonic Service Bureaus.
A Lodge may withdraw from a Masonic Service Bureau for good cause,
subject to the approval of the Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus.
Masonic Service Bureaus shall report annually to the Committee on

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

233

Masonic Service Bureaus showing their work, receipts and
disbursements in detail, specifying separately what, if any, has been
expended for relief of Masons, their widows and orphans, funerals of
deceased Masons, and how much thereof has been repaid. Grand
Lodge will not appropriate any of its funds to any Bureau not so
reporting, and unless it has shown that its actual expenses have been
borne by the Lodges constituting it.
All applications from Masonic Service Bureaus or Lodges for funds or
reimbursement shall be submitted to the Grand Secretary at least sixty
days prior to each Annual Communication of Grand Lodge. The Grand
Secretary shall make a thorough investigation of the merits of each
application and report his findings and recommendations to the
Committee on Finance on or before the opening day of the Annual
Communication. If, in the opinion of the Grand Secretary, good cause
is shown that immediate reimbursement is essential, reimbursement may
be made with the approval of the Grand Master.
As the Institute for Masonic Studies is not part of our Code, no changes
to the Code are required for the Grand Master to assign the duties of the
Masonic Education Committee or Masonic History Committee to that
or any other Grand Lodge entity, including a special committee of the
Grand Lodge, should the Grand Master so decide.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
For several years, this Grand Lodge has been honing and fine tuning its
various committees and boards. Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2 seeks
to restructure several of these committees and boards even further, thereby
streamlining and creating more productive entities. This will result in faster and
easier acquisition of information from these committees and boards.
Consequently there should be a sizable reduction in the overall costs to this
Grand Lodge.
This Recommendation is consistent with the policy and general
purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Report of the Committee on Finance:
Approval of this Recommendation will result in the elimination of eight
committees either by consolidation of various committees or by their duties
being assigned to the Grand Secretary or the Grand Chaplain. While it is
difficult to know the exact financial impact, it is felt that with fewer committees,
there will be fewer committeemen and therefore some reduction in cost.

Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 3
Clarifying Language of Section 7020 of the California Masonic Code
In my Grand Master’s Decision No. 5 I ruled that Section 7020 of the
California Masonic Code had been written in an ambiguous manner when a
Grand Master’s decision under the 1975 code was incorporated in this section.
There are some additional issues with this section which should be addressed. I
therefore recommend that Section 7020 be revised as indicated below.
If you adopt this recommendation, the California Masonic Code will be
amended as marked:
§7020. INVESTMENT.
The funds of Grand Lodge and any entity thereof or under the control of
Grand Lodge shall be separately invested as follows:
A.

Such funds shall be invested with the care, skill, prudence and
diligence under the circumstances then prevailing, including
but not limited to the general economic conditions and the
anticipated needs of the entity whose funds are being invested,
that a prudent person acting in a like capacity and familiar with
such matters would use in the conduct of an enterprise of like
character and with like aims to accomplish the purposes of that
entity.

B.

The specific and detailed guidelines to govern the day-to-day
investment activities of each entity subject to this section shall
be promulgated from time to time by the Grand Lodge
Committee on Investments in consultation with the Grand
Treasurer. Those guidelines applicable to Grand Lodge
investments shall be subject to the approval of the Grand
Master. Those guidelines applicable to the investments of the
Masonic Homes and the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund
shall be subject to the approval of the Board of Trustees of the
Masonic Homes.
Those guidelines applicable to the
investments of the California Masonic Memorial Temple shall
be subject to the approval of the Board of Trustees of that
entity. Those guidelines applicable to the investments of the
California Masonic Foundation shall be subject to the approval
of the Board of Trustees of that entity. Those guidelines
applicable to the investments of Nob Hill Masonic
Management Inc. shall be subject to the approval of the Board

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

235

of Directors of that entity. The guidelines applicable to any
other entity shall be subject to the approval of the Board of
Trustees (or Directors) of that entity.
C.

The various funds of each entity subject to this section may be
pooled for investment purposes, but. nNo funds of any one
entity may be pooled with the funds of any other entity unless
there is a separate accounting and valuation of each entity’s
funds at least annually.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 3 removes ambiguity in the
current language of California Masonic Code Section 7020, the Section of the
Code governing Grand Lodge investments. The current language has caused
some confusion as to whether money from different Masonic entities could be
placed in the same investment vehicle, such as a common stock mutual fund.
This Recommendation makes clear that current Grand Lodge investment
practices, practices that have been used for at least the past six years, are
permitted. The current investment policy permits Grand Lodge to place money
from different entities, such as the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund and
California Masonic Memorial Temple, in the same investment. It is both
convenient and practical to do this, and past experience has shown this practice
to be beneficial. The amounts invested and the return on investment for each
entity is strictly and separately accounted for. Each entity receives investment
income in exact proportion to the amount invested by that entity. The proposed
Recommendation simply clarifies Section 7020 so there is no ambiguity
regarding current Grand Lodge investment policy.
