Resolution No. 02-14
Early Presentation of 50- and 75-Year Pins
The proponents of this Resolution seek to permit early presentation of 50- and
75-year pins.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, our Fraternity appropriately recognizes those members
that have attained the required length of service and are to be recognized for a
fifty-year or a seventy-five year pin and Certificate is most appropriate. This
Resolution is proposed to provide any qualifying Brother who is known or
believed to be terminally ill to request and receive an early ceremony anytime
within 180 days (six months) of his attaining the lawfully required length of
service. Furthermore, it is the intention of this Resolution to accept, without
medical evidence, the honorable word of the requesting Brother of his current
medical condition; and
WHEREAS, presently our Grand Lodge cannot authorize said
presentation until the Brother has fully completed the required time to the exact
prescribed anniversary date and not a day sooner. Many other Jurisdictions
allow the Brother to receive his recognition any time of his choice during that
calendar year as a personal convenience to that Brother and his family. Part of
that time flexibility is related to the fact that the Brother has paid his current dues
and is therefore considered to be “in good standing for that full calendar year”;
and
WHEREAS, all too often we find the deserving Brother has fallen ill or
has passed on just prior to attaining this exacting anniversary date. Often, the
widow or members of the immediate family have requested a pin so he may be
buried with it and the Inspector or Grand Lodge cannot lawfully comply due to
the constraints placed upon them. Our Fraternity is not about restrictions. It is
about compassion, to say the very least.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 23150 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as follows:
§23150

GOLDEN VETERAN
AWARDS.

AND

DIAMOND

JUBILEE

The Grand Lodge, upon request, shall provide a Golden Veteran’s
Award to members of this Jurisdiction who have been in good standing
for a total of 50 years or more, presentation being made by the Grand
Master, District Inspector, or their representative with appropriate
ceremony.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

277

The Grand Lodge, upon request, shall provide a Diamond Jubilee
Award to members of this Jurisdiction who have been in good standing
for a total of 75 years or more, presentation being made by the Grand
Master or his designee with appropriate ceremony.
In the case of a deceased Mason, if he was eligible for but had not
received the Award prior to his death, or in the case of an incompetent
Brother, presentation on request may be made to the family or heirs.
In computing the length of time requisite for issuance of either award, a
period of up to one year, during which the Brother held a Dimit from
any Lodge recognized by this Grand Lodge, may be included; however,
periods during which the Brother held a Dimit for more than one year
from the date of the Dimit, or was suspended for non-payment of dues,
must be added to the time when the Brother would otherwise have been
eligible to receive the award. The period between the issuance of a
Dimit for the purpose of becoming a charter member of a new Lodge
and the institution of the new Lodge is not an interruption of the
required number of years of continuous membership.
A Brother, or, if he be incapacitated, a member of his immediate family,
may request that either award be presented to the Brother at any time
within the calendar year of the Brother’s eligibility, even if such time
predates the date on which the Brother would otherwise have been
eligible.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
California Masonic Code Section 23150 provides that a Golden
Veteran’s Award shall be made upon request to members of this jurisdiction who
have been in good standing for a total of 50 years or more and that a Diamond
Jubilee Award be made on request to members in good standing for a total of 75
years or more.
The ceremony and pin are obtainable solely at the will and pleasure of
the Supreme Architect who grants us these years of life and this achievement
clearly deserves the recognition of our Grand Lodge.
This Resolution requires that the presentation be made in the same
“calendar year” as eligibility creating unintended discrimination. Discrimination
occurs when, as an example, a Brother reaches 50 years of membership in
February 2003; and therefore the earliest presentation in the calendar year
permitted under this Resolution would be January 2003, one month early.
However, if the Brother reaches 50 years of membership in December 2003, that
Brother could also receive his pin in January 2003, eleven months early.
For the forgoing reasons, this Resolution is not consistent with Grand

278

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lodge policy as it would result in discriminatory treatment by our Grand Lodge
to those who have held membership for many years.
Report of the Committee on Finance:
This Resolution would permit the early presentation of the 50 and 75 year
veterans pin and certificate. The cost to Grand Lodge cannot be determined
because the number of pins involved is not known. However, it is felt that the
number of early presentations would be quite small and therefore the cost very
nominal.
Resolution WITHDRAWN.
Resolution No. 02-15
Wearing Officers Jewels Before Opening Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to amend the Ritual to have the Officers
wear the jewel of their office prior to the opening ceremony.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, many Blue Lodges now wear jewels with heavy collars
similar in weight to the collars worn by the Grand Lodge Officers when
traveling; and
WHEREAS, the Blue Lodge now follows the ritual of the Grand Lodge
where applicable; and
WHEREAS, the perambulation of the officers to receive their jewels is
of no ritualistic consequence; and
WHEREAS, this ritual has not distracted from the Grand Lodge’s
opening; and
WHEREAS, the elimination of receiving the officers jewel will not
distract from the Blue Lodge opening ceremony; and
WHEREAS, the ring attaching the jewel to the collar was designed to
hold the weight of the jewel not the weight of the collar thus carrying the collar
with the jewel can cause damage to the ring.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Lodge Officers shall
enter the Lodge room together, already wearing the jewel and apron of their
office, and take their stations prior to opening.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

279

The proponents of this Resolution seek to eliminate the current ritual of
the officers approaching the east to receive their jewels.
They propose that the officers put on their jewels and aprons and enter
the Lodge room in procession and take their stations prior to opening.
This Resolution is similar to Resolution 95-06, which was not adopted.
In its report to the Grand Lodge in 1995, the Ritual Committee stated: “We are,
in general, not in favor of changing a practice that has not proven a burden to the
Lodge over the years. The practice of obtaining the jewel of office from the
Master of the Lodge has some symbolic significance, for it reminds the office
holder that it is the Master of the Lodge that continues to give him the right to
wear the jewel of office. It emphasizes the right of the Master to govern the
Lodge over which he is called to preside, and confirms that the holding of a
Masonic office is a privilege.”
However, another similar Resolution was proposed in 1996 and the
Ritual Committee reported that: “The Resolution expressed the intent of the
Ritual Committee by having the Lodge Officers enter the Lodge room similar to
that demonstrated by the Grand Lodge Officers procession.” This Resolution
was carried over in 1996 and not adopted in 1997.
Based on past history, this Resolution is not consistent with current
Grand Lodge policy.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 02-16
Pledge of Allegiance
The proponents of this resolution seek to add the Pledge of Allegiance to all
Lodge Openings.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the California Masonic Code requires the Flag of our
Country to be displayed at all meetings of Masonic Lodges; and
WHEREAS, the California Grand Lodge Masonic Ritual does not
prescribe that a Pledge of Allegiance to the Flag of the United States be rendered
at all Tiled meetings of Lodges; and
WHEREAS, the citizens of the United States today are undergoing a
renewed sense of national pride and patriotism and respect for our National Flag
and what it represents to freedom-loving people around the world; and

280

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

WHEREAS, the Masonic Fraternities of this Great Nation are among
the most supportive and patriotic citizens of our beloved Country.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that the California Grand
Lodge Committee on Ritual be authorized to require a Pledge of Allegiance to
the Flag of the United States of America, as an integral part of the opening ritual
of all Lodges.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that such requirement be added in the
opening ritual immediately following the Invocation, while the Brethren are still
upstanding.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The Pledge of Allegiance is generally regarded as a statement reflecting
one’s patriotism for this country. The proponents of this Resolution wish to
make the Pledge a mandatory component of every tiled meeting in every Lodge
in California.
There is no uniform policy regarding the occasions when the Pledge is
recited in any jurisdiction in the United States. In California, Section 16100 of
the California Masonic Code requires that the flag be displayed at certain
specified occasions. Under our current practice it is the prerogative of the
Master of any Lodge to have the Pledge recited at any other occasion he believes
it to be appropriate. If he so chooses, the Pledge could be recited at every
meeting. Nothing in our law prevents an individual Lodge from adopting such a
tradition if it so desires.
Words alone do not create patriotism, and patriotism does not need
designated times for expression. This Resolution would only create an
unnecessary requirement for those Lodges and Masons who would otherwise
choose to express their love of country at a different time or place.
This Resolution is not consistent with our current policy.
Report of the Committee on Finance:
This Resolution No. 02-16, if adopted, will result in approximately
$3,000.00 in cost to the Grand Lodge to reprint the Monitor.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Resolution No. 02-17
Eliminate Officer Proficiencies
The proponents of this Resolution seek to eliminate proficiency requirement for
Masters and Wardens.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, many Lodges have a problem filling the stations of the
Master, Wardens and Senior Deacon, due to the requirements of Sections 25070
A & B, 25090 A & B, 25100 A& B of the California Masonic Code. As a result
of this condition, Lodges either turn in their Charter or consolidate with another
Lodge; and
WHEREAS, these consolidations eliminate the Lodge’s presence in
communities, towns and cities.
It increases the consolidated lodge’s
membership, in some cases to more than 1000 members, and most of these
members become inactive due to poor communication within the Lodge; and
WHEREAS, there are many Brothers who have the ability to perform
all the duties required of the Elected and Appointed Officers, as stated in
Sections 25140, 25210 and 25240, but do not have the time or inclination to
qualify as a ritualist; and
WHEREAS, since 1994 it has been proven that when Lodges work
together, the participating ritualists from various Lodges present a very
impressive degree, have larger attendance at the degree, and creates a mutual
feeling of having stretched forth a helping hand. This action assures the
existence of the Lodges and the continued influence of Masonry in communities,
towns and cities;
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Sections 25070, 25080,
25090, and 25100 be amended as follows:
§25070 QUALIFICATIONS OF MASTER.
Before a Master-elect can be installed there shall be presented to the
Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand Lecturer, Assistant Grand
Lecturer of the division in which his Lodge is situated, or the Inspector
of the district in which his Lodge is situated, certifying that: he is
proficient in those portions of this Code that relate to the government of
a Lodge.
A.

He has personally examined the Master-elect and that he is
qualified to give the work and lectures in the three degrees;

B.

He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the Candidate’s

282

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lecture of the Master Mason Degree; and
The ritual work of the Master may be performed by any Master Mason
whom the Lodge’s Officer’s Coach shall have determined to be
qualified to perform the same.
§25080 QUALIFICATIONS OF SENIOR WARDEN.
Before a Senior Warden-elect can be installed there shall be presented
to the Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand Lecturer, Assistant

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

283

§25100 QUALIFICATIONS OF SENIOR DEACON.
Before a Brother can be installed as Senior Deacon there shall be
presented to the Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand Lecturer,
the Assistant Grand Lecturer or the division in which his Lodge is
situated, or the Inspector of the district in which his Lodge is situated,
certifying that:
A.

He has personally examined the Brother; and

B.

He is duly qualified to give the work and Senior Deacon’s
lecture of the Fellow Craft Degree.

The ritual work of Senior Deacon may be performed by any Master
Mason whom the Lodge’s Officer’s Coach shall have determined to be
qualified to perform the same.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to modify the qualifications of
the Master-elect, Senior and Junior Wardens-elect and the Senior Deacon by no
longer requiring them to be proficient in the work of the three degrees. Thus, the
senior officers of the Lodge would not be required to be proficient in any ritual.
These officers would only be required to be proficient in the portions of
the Code that relate to the government of a Lodge.
Additionally, this Resolution would permit any Master Mason whom
the officer’s coach, not the Inspector, deemed qualified to perform the officers’
ritual.
This Committee has stated on numerous occasions that the senior
officers of the Lodge, the Master in particular, are charged with the
responsibility of overseeing all of the activities of the Lodge, including the ritual.
As such, they themselves must be proficient in the ritual. In 1989, this
Committee enunciated this principle by noting that if someone wants to sit in the
East and judge the proficiency level of another, his own knowledge must be
complete and thorough. In 1997, in response to a Resolution intended to exempt
officers from requalifying for two years, the Ritual Committee observed that
holding a certificate attesting that a man was proficient at one time does not
mean that he is proficient now. These positions were again stated in 2000 in
response to Resolution Number 00-08 which sought to automatically qualify an
officer if he was a past master.

284

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The policy of this Grand Lodge has not deviated. The three principal
officers and the Senior Deacon must be proficient in the candidate’s lectures for
which they are responsible.
This resolution is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

288

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Meetings of the Trustees are held on the first Wednesday of the month,
alternating between the Union City and Covina Homes.
The administrators and committee chairmen of the Board of Trustees
contribute in the preparation of this Annual Report as follows:
Admissions Committee
By Gary A. Yanes, Chairman
The Admission Review Program of the Board is a carefully developed
analysis and evaluation process that continues to be improved and refined to
meet the evolving needs of our Applicants, as well as the Homes Staff and the
Board.
The interview process and on site evaluation is done at the same time
the applicant visits one of the two facilities. A Staff report on this overnight
visit, together with the Medical Director’s comments on his/her review of the
applicant’s medical history and data, is then added to the application
information. The staff then develops and executive summary on the applicants
and presents that to the Admission Committee for review and approval. Anytime
the Staff recommends against admission the full application is given to the
Admissions Committee for detailed review and discussion during the Admissions
Committee meeting. Any recommendation against admission requires the full
Board confirmation.
Prior to the regular Admission Committee meeting, on the second
Wednesday of each month, the five Trustees, who form the Admission
Committee, each receive and carefully study the applications not recommended
for Masonic assistance and the executive summaries for applications
recommended for Masonic assistance. Each Trustee reviews and evaluates the
applications and accompanying data, and then discusses his viewpoint during the
Admissions Committee meeting. Homes Staff and the Administrators attend
Admissions Committee meeting to answer committee members’ questions and to
provide additional information to the committee members. The committee’s
recommendations and comments are then presented to the full Board for
confirmation.
The number of applications handled by the Admission Committee for
couples, individuals and children continues to vary form month to month. From
July 1, 2001 to June 30, 2002, 59 residents have been admitted 53 on partial
assignment of assets and 6 on fee for Service). Union City currently has 246
residents (156 on full assignment of assets, 84 on partial assignment of assets,
and 6 on fee for service); Covina currently has 74 adult residents (35 on full
assignment of assets, 37 on partial assignment of assets and 2 on monthly fee).
The Masonic Outreach Services program (formerly known as NonResident Assistance program) continues to grow and is an effective program in

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

289

assisting our eligible Brethren and their families or widows. This program
provides the necessary support, financial, housing and/or care management
assistance to applicants who are in need of this type of assistance to remain in
their present community or residence. The Masonic Outreach Service program
is current serving the need of 72 clients and 2 on outplacement. This year the
committee has reviewed and approved 29 applications for this program.
Members of the staff monitor these individuals on a regular basis to ensure that
their needs are being met.
The Covina facility provides hospice care for its terminally ill residents,
thus giving its residents a palliative care option previously only available
through the placement in a skilled nursing setting. The Board recently approved
the development of a Dementia and Alzhiemer’s unit at our Union City facility
to meet the ever-changing needs of our applicants.
The Child Care Program continues to develop. All of the new housing
for this program is complete and children are moving into the new homes. This
program is now serving the needs of 48 children with 4 on the waiting list for
admission. In 2001, the Child Care Program received certification from Boys
Town acknowledging the Behavior Management Model program currently
employed at the Covina Home.
I sincerely thank the other Trustee members of the Admission
Committee who labor long and hard over the applications. They are William
Boxell, Tom Joy, Ken Nagel and Ernest Pooleon. I also extend my appreciation
to the Administrators of the Covina and Union City Homes and their staff, as
well as the continued dedication of the Masonic Assistance Staff to the
applicants and Trustees.
Medical Advisory Committee
By Ernest A. Pooleon, Chairman
The committee continues to provide a pro-active focus and professional
support body, as well as to ensure that the Residents at the Homes receive the
highest affective level of health care support available. The committee is
committed to assure the Board of Trustees that on-going medical services are
consistent with the best medical practices, to provide advisory assistance and
guidance to the Board of Trustees as required, provide assistance and counsel to
both the Medical Director and Board when “second opinions” are appropriate,
provide advisory assistance and guidance to the Medical Director and
Administrators applicable to on-going issues or problems, as requested by the
Medical Director or Administrators, and to provide counsel and guidance to both
Medical Directors and the board in areas of Long Range Planning for issues,
needs and concerns likely to arise in the future, is reflected in it’s activities
during the past year.
The committee initiated last year and continues modify and fine tune

290

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the evaluation of the following:
Aging in Place Initiative that is designed to allow our residents to
remain in an independent setting longer by reviewing the normal aging process
and factors that accelerate premature aging. The careful monitoring of the
causative factors for the transfer to a hospital has resulted in a 43% reduction for
Covina and a 13% reduction for Union City. The careful monitoring of the
causative factors for the transfer of our residents to the skilled nursing
unit/facility has resulted in a 53% decrease in admission for Covina and a 19%
reduction for Union City.
Skilled Nursing Improvement Indicators for fall prevention,
dehydration and pressure ulcer formation. The results were most impressive and
reflected the high quality of service provided to our residents. Comparisons of
fall prevention/detection, dehydration and weight loss were evaluated for the
skilled nursing, assisted living and independent living.
Utilization of services such as clinic appointments, dental,
ophthalmology and referrals to the acute hospital were reviewed for
appropriateness, timeliness and duration of stay. The result was a greater
awareness of the necessity to carefully monitor the utilization of services and
referrals to outside consultant services and a reduction in numbers of referrals
and thus a reduction in cost to the home.
It is important to note that such reviews and monitoring is an ever ongoing effort to ensure the highest quality of services are provided our residents at
cost that is within an acceptable range.
The Committee continues to evaluate the utilization of a centralized
database for the clinic physicians and an integrated system for medical services
between the campus at Covina and Union City. Protocol has been established to
implement a system of credential validation for all professional staff. There has
been an enhanced review of selected infectious and communicable diseases and
the effect of said diseases on our community population.
The committee looks forward to working closely with our new medical
director Dr. Sheila Raumer, MD, John Howl, our new administrator at Covina
and continuing the fine relationship with Robert M. Fallon, our administrator at
Union City.

Strategic Planning, Finance, and Facilities Committee
By George L. Geanoulis, Chairman
In past years, the Masonic Homes of California had three separate
committees devoted to the issues of Strategic Planning, Facilities and Finance.
Recognizing that these three organizational elements are linked and cannot be
understood except in relation to one another, a single committee was formed and
given oversight responsibility for all three areas. The intent behind the
consolidation of committees was to consider all initiatives and projects in terms
of their relation to the organization’s short and long-term strategic priorities and
financial goals.
Through this consolidation, the Strategic Planning, Facilities and
Finance Committee was charged with the responsibility of stewarding the
organization’s key resources. This includes spending our charitable dollars
wisely to provide maximum relief while protecting our resources for the future.
As Trustees of the Homes -- these living monuments of masonic charity --we
serve on this Committee to fulfill the vision of the past and to make new
promises to the future.
The committee’s priorities, challenges and opportunities in the areas of
strategic planning, facilities and finance are summarized below.
STRATEGIC
Priorities
Wisely managing our resources to secure the organization’s ability to
meet the needs of current and future generations
Strengthening the relationship with the lodges to ensure they are given
the resources and support they need to live up to their responsibility to
their individual members
Increasing our outreach and communication to the membership as a
whole to ensure that all those in need are being served. This includes a
renewed commitment to seeking out our most vulnerable and aged
members.
Understanding and responding to the needs of our fraternal family
Challenges
Serving the increased demands of the current generation while
protecting our assets for the future
An unprecedented aging of our membership that has led to an expanded
definition of “distress”. As more of our members enter into very
advanced age, ‘need’ is defined much more broadly than purely
economical and can include such factors as isolation, lack of needed

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

293

services and other psychosocial issues.
Maintaining an equitable system of care that serves individuals across
the economic continuum
Lack of information about our widows – with a result that the most
vulnerable are also the most disenfranchised members of our fraternal
family
Insufficient communication between the lodges and the Homes resulting
in a widening of the cracks through which those in need can fall
Opportunities
Completing and implementing a detailed communications and outreach
plan to improve our connection to the lodges and identify and reach out
to those in need
Exploring alternative delivery and financing methods to position
ourselves to meet the evolving and emerging needs of the membership
Incorporating the work of the Alternative Retirement Living Committee
into the short and long-term strategic plan for the Homes.
FINANCE
Priorities
In partnership with the Investment Committee, implementing an
investment and savings plan that meets the immediate needs of our
membership and protects the resources for the future
Oversight of the financial management of the Homes’, including
appropriately budgeting and funding operations and capital
improvements
Identifying improved and more efficient ways of providing service
Challenges
Simultaneously operating under new spending guidelines and projecting
an increased demand for our services.
Achieving targeted cost reductions in operations while maintaining and
improving quality
Meeting the needs of today’s generation while preparing for the needs
of tomorrow’s
Opportunities
Partnerships with organizations with similar and compatible missions

294

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Exploring alternative revenue and funding sources
Researching alternative delivery methods and programs that provide
high quality services in a cost-effective way
FACILITIES
Priorities
Protecting our resources for the future
Developing a long-term campus master plan for each of our facilities
Completing a comprehensive asset management schedule to set the
framework of our capital needs for the future
Completing the renovations on the two campuses on time and on budget
Completing the construction of the new children’s Homes on the
Covina campus
Achieving full entitlements to develop our land on both campuses
Determining the appropriate direction for the proposed golf course
project
Understanding the accommodation preferences and expectations of the
membership
Challenges
Capital constraints
Striking the right balance in our investment in “bricks and mortar”
Opportunities
Exploring opportunities to meet members’ preference for Masonicsponsored services in their home communities
Using the framework established by the campus master plan to fully
entitle our property
Completing feasibility study on the proposed golf course and determine
what, if anything, should be done with the property
This Committee and its Chairman would like to acknowledge the
leadership and support of the entire Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes
and its President, Frederick Sorsabal. Together we have endeavored to make the
best and highest possible use of the resources entrusted to us. We are committed
to upholding the standards of excellence demanded of us by the tradition of
freemasonry and remain inspired and motivated by the words spoken over 30

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

years ago by Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful Theodore Meriam:
“I want to challenge each of you to do your personal part in
adding to the wonderful heritage that is ours in Freemasonry.
We are prone to be complacent; and to take things for granted.
Freemasonry must never be stagnant; rather, it must always be
advancing. We must always be striving for improvement in all
that we do.”
President’s Report
by Frederick L. Sorsabal
As detailed in the various Committee and Staff reports, it has been a
fruitful and productive year for the Masonic Homes of California. Many
projects conceived years ago were completed and surpassed all expectations,
such as the new homes for children on the Covina campus and the renovations
and additions on both adult campuses. In addition, many new projects were
launched in response to emerging and pressing needs, such as the creation of a
new 16 bed Alzheimers/Dementia Unit on the Union City campus and expanded
outreach services.
Since changing our fee structure in 2000, demand for our services has
steadily risen. We now have waiting lists of more than one year at each of our
Homes for adults. With these waiting lists, it has become apparent that those on
the lists must be provided interim support and this is being accomplished through
our Masonic Outreach Program.
At the Children’s Home in Covina, we have eight (8) new dwellings
that each house 6 – 7 children in addition to the Residential Specialists.
Currently, the program serves 48 children between the ages of 4 – 17 with three
children on the waiting list. Each child is sponsored by one of our Masonic
Lodges. In a ceremony hosted by our Grand Master, C. Ray Whitaker, the
Homes were rededicated in a traditional Masonic Rededication & Renewal
Ceremony on June 28, 2002 with over 600 Masons and guests in attendance.
As a living testament to the lives that are being changed by our Masonic
philanthropy, the current residents of the Children’s Home are participating in
this year’s Annual Communication for the first time. One of the children will
speak on life in the Homes and all the children will participate in the Homes
Informational booth during the session. Through them, the face of Masonic
charity comes alive.
This year we have challenged ourselves to continually improve our
services and understanding of the needs of our fraternal family. We organized
our efforts into four key goal areas:
•

Quality Improvement

296

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

•

Strategic Performance and Program Development

•

Organization

•

Performance and Cost Improvement

In addition to the completion of many projects and the launching of new
initiatives, we spent considerable time and resources learning about the needs of
the membership. We held a series of focus groups with our members across the
State and researched social and care issues facing seniors across California.
We are at a challenging point in the history of our fraternity in this state
in that an unprecedented number of us are over the age of 80. Many of our
members are adapting to fixed and /or limited incomes, chronic medical
conditions and the increasing need for assistance with the activities of daily
living. Many members have minimal or no family support and would be isolated
without the support of Masonry.
In 1900, the average lifespan for Americans was 49 years. Now over
100 years later, the average life span is 75 years. With this has come a range of
age-related disorders and issues relatively unknown when the Homes were
founded. For example, it is estimated that 50% of those 85 years old have some
form of dementia – in 1900 there were so few individuals that were 85 years old
that the disease was incidental compared to today’s epidemic.
In response to this emerging need, a new 16 bed dedicated
alzheimers/dementia facility will open later this year on the Union City campus.
Given the number of individuals within our fraternity who are facing this
physically, emotionally and financially draining disease, the Board of Trustees
will continue to seek out and offer the greatest relief possible to those within the
Craft who fall victims to this disease.
The Board of Trustees recently made a commitment to increase our
outreach to another vulnerable segment of our fraternal family – our elderly
widows. We know through Lodge consolidations and poor record keeping that
we have lost track of many of our widows. We also know that these widows are
at risk of “slipping through the cracks” and spending their final years isolated,
impoverished, and lonely. That is, without the support of our Brotherhood. It is
a painful irony that the most vulnerable members of our fraternal family are also
the most disenfranchised. We must not forget our obligation to these wonderful
people.
As we surveyed the constellation of challenges confronting our aging
members, it is clear that without the support of our fraternity, many would
struggle and be alone with their struggles. To prevent any of our members from
facing this kind of isolation, the Homes are now challenging the lodges to make
a renewed commitment to the most aged and vulnerable members of our fraternal
family. This commitment can include such things as a monthly phone call, a
drive to the physician or a referral to the Homes. The Homes helps support the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

297

lodges in their responsibilities by offering information and referral about senior
services in their area. If the Lodge finds that the needs of an individual member
are greater than the Lodge’s ability to support, those needs may be met either in
the Homes or through the Outreach Program. Only through the combined efforts
of individual members, lodges and staff of Grand Lodge and the Masonic Homes
can ensure no eligible member of our fraternal family goes unheeded or unheard.
In June, the Homes Board of Trustees held a three-day Strategic
Planning Retreat to wrestle with how to best live out our mission in today’s
world and protect our resources for the generations of the future. The issues
raised were complex and challenging and warranted further discussion.
Accordingly, I formed an “Alternative Retirement Task Force” to finish this
work and report back to the Board of Trustees in January 2003.
The next step will be to finalize the organization’s strategic plan, which
will help ensure that we effectively meet the responsibilities imposed on us as
Trustees of the Homes and its resources – being ever mindful that these
resources represent the collective generosity and visions of the Masons in
California.
I would like to take this opportunity to express my appreciation to each
Board Member for their commitment to excellence and the cause of
Freemasonry. My special thanks to the members who took on the added
responsibility of chairing our important committees, each gave a great deal of
their time to see that their charge was carried out. The hard work and dedication
of each and every Trustee has helped us achieve our goals and set a sound course
for the future.
On a closing note, Worshipful Brother Ralph Head, Past Grand
Treasurer and Editor Emeritus of The California Freemason, has been a
tremendous supporter of the Masonic Homes of California and to reflect Ralph’s
tireless and selfless contributions for Masonry, that glorious red brick building
on the Union City campus has been renamed the “Ralph Head Administration
Building”. This will serve as a living reminder to us all to live by the example
he set and to devote ourselves as passionately to the principles of Freemasonry.

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
September 2002
The Masonic Homes of California achieved virtually all of the goals
outlined by the Board of Trustees for Fiscal Year 2002. These objectives were
attained through the concerted efforts of an increasingly cohesive and effective
leadership team. John Howl joined the team as the new Executive Director
(Administrator) of Covina; Steffani Kizziar assumed the role of Director of
Program Development, replacing a prior Marketing role; David Hamilton
accepted the role of Director of Finance. Each of these leaders brings extensive
management experience in health care, including long term care. Having
invested in a talented leadership team at the senior and middle management
levels, we began to foster their effectiveness through training and team building.
This was a milestone year for establishing the foundations for a
comprehensive strategic plan for the Masonic Homes. Additional research on
the preferences and service needs of the Fraternity was conducted. Campus
master plans were completed that provide a 20-year framework for development
options for the existing campuses. The Board Strategic Planning retreat held in
June, 2002 was one of the most productive and successful in recent history –
providing a Board prioritization of programs and services as well as establishing
a new Committee to study future models for retirement living.
Finally all these objectives were accomplished while achieving (in some
cases exceeding) goals for improved cost effectiveness. Cost per unit of service
has decreased in all areas except the expanded Covina Children’s program even
as we maintained the highest level of quality. Even after these cost reductions,
we continue to exceed industry standards in terms of staffing and service levels.
During FY 2001-2002, the management teams at the Masonic Homes of
California concentrated on accomplishing goals relating to four areas of
organizational performance: Quality Improvement, Strategic Performance and
Program Development, Organizational Development, and Performance and Cost
Improvement. The highlights of these accomplishments are outlined below:
I. QUALITY IMPROVEMENT
Initiatives focused on improving the quality of the services we provide with
a corresponding positive impact on the quality of life of our residents and
clients.
Following implementation of the Homes’ expanded payment options in
January, 2001, both campuses became fully occupied early in the fiscal year.
Detailed information about occupancy statistics is included in the reports from
the Administrators of each campus.
We continue to focus on enhancing the “aging in place” and “wellness”
programs for our residents, ensuring that they remain independent and active for

300

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

as long as possible. The percentage of residents at both campuses being cared
for in nursing facilities (on campus at Union City and off campus at Covina)
declined as more residents are supported at an assisted living level. Acute
hospitalizations also declined despite the increased resident population at both
facilities.
Responding to feedback from residents obtained from satisfaction
surveys, we initiated several programs to enhance resident communication and
involvement in the activities of the Homes. Resident meetings were held
regularly with organized agendas, minutes and written follow-up on issues.
Attendance at monthly resident meetings increased by 40% at Covina and 50%
at Union City. The role and importance of resident councils at both campuses
were strengthened. Resident newsletters were expanded with increased resident
involvement in their publication. Finally residents participated in both the
development of campus master plans for both facilities and in the interviewing of
a new Executive Director (Administrator) at Covina.
This year intergenerational programs were started at both facilities: at
Union City, a tutoring and special event program with the Guy Emmanuel
Elementary School; at Covina, the establishment of house grandparents for the
Children’s program as well as creation of a new DeMolay group. These
programs respond to resident requests for more interchange with other
generations.
Our high occupancy at both facilities with applicant waiting lists
approaching almost two years, necessitated continued streamlining of the
application process and better communication with prospective residents while
they await admission. While on the waiting lists, future residents are contacted
at least monthly to maintain updated information about their health status.
Where appropriate, they are supported by the staff of the Masonic Outreach
Program either financially and/or socially.
We surveyed new residents regarding their satisfaction with revised
admission process twice in FY 2002, with the results showing an increase from
85% -90% high satisfaction levels between the two surveys. Management has
incorporated the suggestions arising out of the survey into further refinements of
our systems, including establishing a new system for tracking the status of asset
distribution to residents admitted under partial asset assignment.
The demand for Masonic Outreach Services increased this year as the
waiting list for admission increased. Client volume is projected to rise from 75
in FY 2002 to 100 in FY 2003. This year new procedures and standards for
client follow-up and visits were established. The frequency of client contact
increased according to the clients need. We instituted client wellness services,
including annual physicals and LifeLine connection (“LifeFone”), for Outreach
clients. We also expanded our information dissemination to the Fraternity this
year about this program, including providing each Lodge with a Resource

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

301

Manual on community health and social services available in their communities.
II. STRATEGIC PERFORMANCE AND PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT
Initiatives focused on responding to the evolving needs of the membership
and positioning our organization to remain competitive and strong for the
future.
Conceptual master plans for future space development on both
campuses were finalized and approved by the Board of Trustees. These plans
serve as a framework for potential future development of two existing campuses
over 15-20 year timeframe, including projecting major capital needs.
This year the Board of Trustees continued its evaluation of the possible
development of a golf course on the Union City campus, formulating a more
detailed budget for completion of the project. While we have yet to make a
decision to proceed with the project, there are three new options for golf course
design at regulation length (70 par and above) which appear to be feasible from
an economic and market perspective.
We have fostered a strong collaborative working relationship with City
staff of Union City which should position the organization favorably as we
pursue both the golf course project and other Union City campus development.
The golf course project remains a very exciting one with the Union City City
Council and City staff. The master plan was for the Union City campus in
concept to the City staff.
It is our hope that we can negotiate an allencompassing development agreement with Union City for our northern campus,
including golf course and master plan implementation.
In a continuing effort to enhance dissemination of information about the
Homes and Masonic Outreach to the Blue Lodges, 40 presentations and 16
articles appeared in California Freemason in FY 2002. These efforts contributed
to the increased rate of applications for admission and Masonic Outreach.
In order to continue to understand the needs and demands of the
Fraternal membership better, numerous membership focus groups were
conducted that provided additional information that will be useful in evaluating
options to expand capacity on existing campuses and/or satellite locations
In FY 2002 there was extensive collaboration between the Homes
Board of Trustees and the Grand Lodge Investment Committee to clarify their
respective roles in determining operational and strategic spending levels. The
Board of Trustees adopted Investment Committee recommendations for
distribution guidelines for investment funds: to frame operational and capital
spending for current operations and capital requirements at 5% of the investment
fund balances over a five year moving average. The Homes management team
developed an operating and capital budget for FY 2003 for Board approval that
complied with these distribution targets.

302

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Board of Trustees held an outstanding two-day retreat in Shell
Beach in early June. Much of the discussion centered around improved
understanding of the needs of the Fraternity that have arisen out of recent
research and focus group results. The Board completed a prioritization of 20
potential initiatives and services, which will serve as the basis for staff goals for
the coming fiscal year. An additional outcome of the retreat a new Alternative
Retirement Living Committee was established which will:
•

Explore and define the needs of our membership and the role
of the Homes in meeting those needs,

•

Explore options and opportunities to extend the Homes’
services, and

•

Report to the Board of Trustees by January, 2003 on future
options in addressing appropriate alternative service delivery
structures to meet membership needs and demands.

Management also completed comprehensive evaluations of the following
programs for Board review: an Alzheimer’s Transition Unit, the in-house
Pharmacy (Union City) and Masonic Outreach Lifeline program (“Lifefone”). A
16-bed, assisted living level Alzheimer’s/Dementia unit is currently under
construction in one of the assisted living wings of Union City and slated to open
in November, 2002.
III. ORGANIZATION
Initiatives focused on strengthening, educating and improving the staff,
management and executive teams and Board of Trustees to increase job
satisfaction and quality of services provided.
A management development program was started at both campuses,
with a 30 hour middle management training program provided for 16 managers.
Each campus held joint executive-middle management team-building retreats to
review our organizational vision and formulate future goals and priorities.
The Board completed its first self-evaluation questionnaire in October,
2001. Based on the results, the Board Development committee outlined priority
needs for on-going Board education. Trustees participated in Board educational
sessions on several topics, including: financial statements, sources of revenue,
regulatory requirements, and Alzheimer’s/dementia. A number of Board
members also participated in conferences and site visits to other facilities.
An employee recognition program was designed and implemented
which specifically honors employees who demonstrate commitment to the core
Masonic values: MVP (Masonic Values Program) in their work and personal
interactions.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

303

IV. PERFORMANCE AND COST IMPROVEMENT
Initiatives focused on identifying improved or more efficient ways of
providing service and ensuring that we remain responsible stewards of our
resources.
The organization achieved annualized savings in operating expenses of
over $1,000,000, exceeding the targeted savings. This was done out of
recognition that the cost structure of the Homes had grown to exceed standards
in the long-term care industry. While we strive to always provide the highest
level of services to our residents, Board and management recognize that being
prudent stewards of our financial resources allows our charitable purpose to be
extended to more recipients. The most significant source of the savings came
from reducing the number of residents receiving higher levels of skilled nursing
care by increasing the resources available to them at the assisted living level of
care. Not only does this result in cost effectiveness, it also enhances resident
independence and satisfaction.
There has been a slight increase in the average asset base of new
residents as a result of the new entrance fee options. As a result, the reliance on
investment earnings as the predominant source of covering operating expenses of
the Homes was reduced from 90% to 80% during the last year. Nevertheless,
the vast majority of residents still choose to enter the Homes under the partial
assignment of assets option rather than the fee for service option and their care is
substantially subsidized by the proceeds of the Homes Endowment.
We were pleased that we were able to comply with more disciplined
spending standards that were recommended by the Grand Lodge Investment
Committee. Over the next year we will continue to evaluate and streamline our
systems and procedures with a focus on better service and improved
productivity.
Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE ADMINISTRATOR
MASONIC HOME, UNION CITY
2001 - 2002
The fiscal year 2001-2002 was a year of significant changes for the
Masonic Homes at Union City. Our overall goal was to streamline operation
costs while maintaining quality services. As stewards overseeing the financial
components of the whole facility, management studied other high quality
retirement communities and implemented staffing ratios to meet the highest
quality standards in the industry.
We have established a strong management team with the addition of an
Assistant Administrator in Health Services, a new Medical Director and a new
Director of Facility Services. We will continue to evaluate our services and
make sure we have the best management team to do the job. The Home has been
active informing the craft of the services we provide through articles in the
Freemason magazine. Presently we are committing four (4) pages to every issue
of the Freemason.
Involvement in the local community has been very important for the
Masonic Homes. We initiated an intergenerational program with the local
elementary school, Guy Emmanual, where students and our residents interact
with each other through classes, programs, field trips and special events. Two
special events highlighted the year; a Christmas Tree Lighting ceremony with
our 100 foot Norway Pine Tree was held in December at the home and an Easter
Egg Hunt at the school in April. We will continue our interaction with Guy
Emmanual School into and through the next school year.
The plans for the Home this coming fiscal year includes a renovation of
the dining room, which will include new carpet, chairs, tables, lighting, wall
coverings and overall layout of the dining room. We have and will continue to
upgrade the kitchen equipment to meet the dining needs of the residents.
Initial plans are complete and construction will begin this fall on the
Masonic Homes’ first Alzheimer/Dementia unit located on the fifth floor of the
Wollenberg Building. This new secured unit will be able to care for 15 residents
who require special treatment for Alzheimer or Dementia. Previously, residents
who developed Alzheimer or Dementia were sent to outside facilities. The
opening date of this new unit will be November 1, 2002 and we will then be able
to care for those residents on our own campus.
During this past fiscal year, we have created the foundation to establish
a new Resident Council for the home at Union City. This council will consist of
seven (7) elected residents who will represent the entire resident community.
They will meet monthly with Administration and be a mediator for resident to

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

305

resident disputes. The election for this new resident council will occur in
September of 2002.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, COVINA
2001 - 2002
It is with a real sense of enthusiasm and excitement that I write my first
annual report to you the Fraternity of the Masons of California. Let me begin by
saying how pleased and honored I am to join the leadership team of Masonic
Homes of California. Upon finishing the school year, my wife Cindy and our two
boys, Paul and Steven, moved from Denver, CO. to join me at the Covina
Campus. While moving is always a challenge; we feel very blessed to be a part
of a mission-driven organization that has a rich history and an exiting future in
the care and service for senior adults and children.
In reflecting on the challenges and successes of our campus this past
year; it is clear, we have a great deal for which to celebrate and be thankful. As
you read this annual report, we especially give thanks for the visionary
leadership of our board of trustees, the loving care of our dedicated staff, and the
tremendous support of volunteers from within our resident population, as well as
our surrounding Lodges and community. The following is a summary of the
fiscal year’s milestones, activities and initiatives.
•

A memorable rededication event was held on June 28, 2002 to celebrate
the renewed commitment of Masonic Homes of California to care for
children in need. This milestone event represents the culmination of a
multi-year $11 million building program, featuring eight custom
designed homes to serve 56 children and accommodate staff.
Approximately 600 guests enjoyed a full day of festivities including a
luncheon, Masonic ceremony and a Hawaiian luau with authentic
Polynesian dancers. Grand Master C. Ray Whitaker presided over the
events of the day; and he and his wife Joyce were presented beautiful
commemorative gifts from our children and staff.

•

In the spring of 2002, the Boys Town National Resource and Training
Center re-certified our children’s program.
This prestigious
certification is granted to foster care and childcare organizations across
the country that complete a rigorous site review to assure they are
meeting or exceeding the Boys Town Standards of Quality Care.

•

Over the course of the year our campus has served 53 children
representing 27 boys and 26 girls. Our children range in age from 4 to
18 years and represent a diversity of ethnic backgrounds, with a variety
of social and health needs. While nearly fifty percent of our children
have a Masonic affiliation at the time of admission; a Masonic Lodge
sponsors each child. Our children are also actively involved in
Rainbow Girls, Jobs Daughters, and Demolay along with a variety of
community service organizations, such as the Midnight Mission. We

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

315

recently subscribed to CompuCare, a special childcare web-based
system that will assist us in tracking and studying the clinical, social,
and academic indicators for our children. We expect this new tracking
and research system will help us to better understand the difference we
are making in the lives of the children we serve.
•

In addition to serving children, our childcare staff has developed
specialty-parenting classes to assist the parents and guardians of our
children, as well as parents from our local community. We are hopeful
this training will not only reinforce our family model of care during
family visits; but also will teach and foster the necessary skills and
commitment to reunite families.

•

On the senior service side of our campus, we presently serve an average
population of 75 seniors; representing the largest senior population in
our campus history. We continue to provide an “ aging in place” model
of care that promotes independence and wellness; while avoiding
unnecessary skilled nursing placement. Given the variety of pricing
options and contract alternatives available to prospective residents; we
also have developed a multi-year waiting list. Thanks to the assistance
of our Masonic Outreach Program, a number of our waiting list
members receive transitional support until an apartment is available for
them.

•

While it is difficult to talk about death and dying; you should know we
have expanded our continuum of care to include hospice services.
Through collaborating with area hospice programs, our residents may
receive comfort care in their apartment during their last days and hours,
with family by their side.

•

In furthering our wellness initiative, a number of our senior adult
residents attend senior wellness classes at Citrus College. With the
assistance of skilled instructors and trainers, our residents are working
out with the latest fitness equipment, swimming laps in an Olympic size
pool, and studying the importance of a nutritional diet. They also enjoy
being back on a college campus.

•

In an effort to consistently provide high quality services for our
residents, we conducted a professional resident satisfaction survey in
May 2002. Through resident and family feedback, we identified our
service strengths and weaknesses as well as opportunities for
improvement. We also compared our results to industry benchmarks to
better understand our competitive advantages and disadvantages.
While our general resident satisfaction is positive, there is room for
improvement. At this time we are in the process of developing
strategies and action plans to assure we are meeting the needs and

316

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

expectations of our residents. As a side note, we continue to be
affirmed with outstanding survey results from our licensing agencies.
•

Our campus continues to serve as the host site for the Masonic Student
Assistance Program. As part of our outreach and mission, we also open
our campus and facilities to Masonic affiliated groups, alumni, and
community groups. We have been blessed with a beautiful campus; and
feel compelled to share it with others.

•

As we look to the future, considerable time has been given to updating
the conceptual master plan for our campus. Our plans represent
feedback from focus groups, residents, staff, and industry professionals.
Our vision includes redecorating, renovation, introducing specialty
areas of care for memory impairment, and expanding independent
living to serve our growing waiting list of senior adults. The prospects
of caring for the diverse needs of Masons are very exciting.

In closing you will see a census list of all the children and senior adults
we have served this past year. My hope and prayer is that as a Fraternity, you
will sense our steadfast commitment as a campus to carry out the enduring
mission of Masonic Homes of California. “To provide quality care to California
Master Masons, their wives, widows and mothers as well as to children with and
without Masonic affiliations, with dignity and compassion, within an
atmosphere, based on Masonic tenets, that fulfill the physical, social, spiritual
and nurturing needs of our extended community.”
Respectfully submitted
John E. Howl, Executive Director

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Most Worshipful, Grand Lodge Officers, Past Grand Masters, Past
Grand Officers, distinguished visitors, members of the Grand Lodge and my
brethren.
It is with great pride that I am able to update you on the activities of the
Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation. This is my fifth year as
president, and I extend my thanks to each of the board members for their
confidence in me, for their dedicated service to our fraternity and to each of you
for your continued support.
The board meets quarterly, twice in the Covina home and twice in the
Grand Lodge Office in order to fulfill the mission of the Foundation: to support
the youth of our country as they strive to become productive citizens.
The theme for this Annual Communication is “Communication: Key to
Education”. The California Masonic Foundation provides the keys to unlock the
door of education for the young people of our state. The first key is
scholarships. The second is support of public schools. The third is the Masonic
Student Assistance Program.
My brethren, because you believe in this great fraternity and have
supported the activities of the California Masonic Foundation, we have made it
possible for teachers to do the job for which they were trained. We have given
them the tools to identify students at risk and the skills to provide the necessary
interventions for successful learning experiences. This year 7,443 students were
referred to a student assistance team trained in Covina through the MSAP
program. Four thousand nine hundred sixty-one of those referred received some
type of intervention. During the past eight years of our program we now have
trained 3,387 educators in 885 different schools or districts, and they have
received 31,407 referrals for assistance. Special help was provided to 16,494
students. We thank you for your generous support of our program.
The Foundation has made the following expenditures this year:
1.

$10,000.00 was given to assist the National Masonic Foundation in
the expansion of the Masonic Student Assistance Program
throughout the United States. Because we are recognized as
having the most outstanding program nationwide, on Friday and
Saturday, October 12 and 13, we hosted a conference of the
administrators, coordinators and leaders from each of the states in
which the program is used. The conference was so successful that
this year we will be the guests of North Carolina to further share
information for the improvement of our offerings.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

323

2.

$2,500.00 was presented to each of the four youth groups for use in
their leadership training, which they could not provide without our
support.

3.

Certificates of Recognition and $1,000.00 checks to each of the
five “California Teachers of the Year” to be used in their respective
school districts as they wish. These presentations were made in the
teacher’s own community thereby affording us the best public
relations program possible. Each one was awarded by a member of
the Foundation Board of Trustees along with the Grand Master or

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Center are pleased to
make the 47th annual report to the Grand Lodge on the condition and
maintenance of this stately edifice. This report is a general overview of the
activities for the past year. A more detailed account is recorded in the minutes of
the quarterly meetings of the Board of Trustees filed in the Grand Lodge office,
and may be viewed by contacting the Grand Secretary.
The overall condition of the California Masonic Memorial Center
(CMMC) is excellent, thanks to the close working relationship this Board has
with the Nob Hill Masonic Management Board (NHMM) in which we share in
the upgrading and maintenance of the physical and mechanical assets we
manage. The costs of operating and maintaining this building are totally borne
by CMMC and NHMM. At no time is monies of The Grand Lodge or per capita
assessment used to operate and maintain your Masonic Center. Actually, funds
are periodically transferred to Masonic Homes and to Grand Lodge by both
Boards.
This past year we have experienced a shortage of energy together with
an unprecedented increase in energy rates. Steps were taken to reduce the
consumption of energy by modifying the lighting, reducing the demand made by
large motors used in the air conditioning and elevators, and by the cooperation of
all personnel using the building to be “energy conscious”.
The dastardly acts by the terrorist of September 11th upon this Nation
has sharpened everyone’s attention to security. A security consulting firm was
engaged to assess the risks to which this facility was exposed. It is quite evident
that it is impossible to protect against every potential situation, never-the-less
action must be taken to protect the occupants and users of this facility to the best
of our ability. We have therefore put into motion a security project designed to
monitor and control the movement of visitors to this facility. This project will be
ongoing during the ensuing year.
The beautiful endomosaic window which adorns the lobby, is the first
such window designed by Emile Norman which is exposed to the outside
atmosphere. Mr. Norman began his design in 1956 and completed the window
in 1958. The mural depicts Masonic wayfarers who came to California in the
1850s. The center of the mural depicts the All-seeing eye and the jewels of the
seven officers, the Master Mason of the present day and appropriate Masonic
symbols. It is a one-of-a-kind work of art which has been appraised at $729,000.
Unfortunately, time, heat, ultraviolet rays and wind have taken their toll on this
artwork, necessitating some action of restoration be taken to preserve it. One of

326

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the 45 panels comprising the mosaic window has been removed for restoration.
This work will enable the restoration workers to evaluate the project. Also under
consideration is the rebuilding of the windscreen behind the mural which
protects the artwork from wind and ultraviolet rays. The windscreen has lost its
filtering effect over years requiring replacement to preserve the window.
The historic sites, buildings and cemeteries are under the control of the
CMMC. One site in particular is the Masonic Hall at El Pueblo de Los Angeles.
Two years ago the city of Los Angeles closed the area for restoration work,
requiring the vacating of the Masonic Hall until the work was completed. A subcommittee chaired by Trustee Scott Porter has been active in working with the
city of Los Angeles to have the restoration completed, and allow the Masons to
re-occupy the Hall. We are pleased to report that the work is nearing
completion. We are optimistic that the Masons will soon be able to once again
meet in this historic building.
The Library/Museum sub-committee under the guidance of Worshipful
Sean Foran has spent many long hours in recording every book title on
computer, acquiring new display cases, rearranging the display cases and
bookshelves and producing story boards to depict the history of Freemasonry in
California from the 1850s to the present day. A wealth of artifacts from all over
the globe are in the inventory of the Library/Museum, with many of them on
display. The Library/Museum is staffed by dedicated volunteers Monday through
Friday, and each day during the Grand Lodge Annual Communication. I urge
each of you to visit the Library/Museum during your stay in San Francisco.
A great deal of thanks goes to the Trustees and Advisors who freely
dedicate their time and effort to the accomplishments of this Board. We are
particularly thankful for the time, experience and knowledge amassed by the
Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper and his capable staff, who without a moment’ s
hesitation, willingly assist the Board in any task they undertake.
Most Worshipful Grand Master Ray Whitaker, on behalf of the Board
of Trustees and Advisors of the California Masonic Memorial Center, I thank
you for allowing us to serve this Grand Lodge.
Respectfully submitted,
J. Lloyd Clayton, President
Robert J. Winston, Vice President

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS OF
NOB HILL MASONIC MANAGEMENT, INC.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

327

To The Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
As President, it is my responsibility and privilege to present, on behalf
of the Board of Directors, the tenth annual report of Nob Hill Masonic
Management, Inc. (Nob Hill).
A decade ago, Nob Hill was established by the Grand Lodge to lease
and operate, for-profit, the California Masonic Center’s auditorium, parking
garage, exhibit hall, conference rooms and various rental properties from the
California Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT), the owners of this Center. (As
an aside, Nob Hill fully supports Grand Lodge action to change the name of
CMMT to CMMC.).
This report is for the fiscal year July 1, 2001 through June 30, 2002 and
will include limited financial and operating highlights for the year just
completed. As you are aware, full, complete, detailed and audited Financial
Statements are available for review and consideration in the appendix of the
Annual Proceedings. I encourage you to review Nob Hill’s financial reports
together with all of the financial reports for the several organizations, which,
taken together, comprise your Grand Lodge.
During the past year, the economy was no kinder to Nob Hill than it
was to the rest of America’s event, entertainment and convention business. We
too went from record profits the previous year to a $70,000 bottom line loss after
taxes, dividend, investment market value adjustments, and depreciation. In spite
of this down economic year, we met the commitment made to you last October,
th
and prior to June 30 , Nob Hill paid Grand Lodge a $90,000 dividend.
Indeed it was a difficult year with revenues dropping 15 to 20% below
our reduced expectations. The drop in revenue forced us to reduce operating
costs even as we maintained the Center, and it’s operating plant and equipment,
at the usual highest of standards. The Center you are in today reflects those
standards. The Maintenance Program is an on-going concern and constant
challenge as this facility approaches its fiftieth year of operation. We have in
place an intensive and comprehensive maintenance program structured to ensure
that this beautiful Masonic Memorial Center will serve the fraternity for another
four or five decades.
Now allow me to report on the major project of this past year. The
nature of terrorism makes it impossible to predict what might or might not
happen. And although the possibility of an attack here on Nob Hill is not high, it
is not zero. So we must and we did take certain carefully considered, and fully
evaluated cost-effective precautions, for the security of all who work at or visit
this facility.
The most significant project undertaken was to implement a facilitywide security plan (mentioned briefly in last year’s report). That substantial

328

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

security project is nearing completion. It required one-time investments of
$157,000 by CMMT and $36,000 by Nob Hill. In addition, Nob Hill incurred
an ongoing annual security system maintenance and personnel cost of $60,000 a
year. As with all security systems and procedures, the exact details are properly
shared on a need-to-know basis.
That said, you cannot but be aware of the security guard in the lobby
with a console that obviously accesses several dozen CCTV cameras located
throughout the Auditorium, Exhibit Hall, Library, Grand Lodge Offices and all
levels of the Garage. Security was a high priority and an absolute requirement
th
after September 11 , and as a public facility we responded appropriately.
As a footnote to the security system work, please note the improved
perimeter lighting on all five levels of the parking garage.
Aside from the security system, all other major projects (such as stage
dimmers, Otis Elevator upgrades and auditorium seat repair and recovery), were
moved back at least a year. They will be individually evaluated and rescheduled as soon as revenues improve and funds become available.
Because many of you are responsible for the Mason Centers in your
local communities, I thought it would be helpful to share a few financial statistics
from this past year’s operation:
•

Rent, taxes, insurance and outside audit fees were $850,000
(Approx. 36% of all expenses)

•

Staffing, including related benefits were $617,000 (Approx. 26%
of all expenses)

•

Utilities: Electric, Gas, & Trash were $171,000 (Approx. 7% of all
expenses)

•

Dividend was paid at $90,000 (Approx. 4% of all expenses)

•

Outside support services were $118,000 (Approx. 5% of all
expenses)

You’ll note that these five categories represent 78% of our budget.
Such a high percentage of unavoidable, or fixed costs, confirms the absolute
value and necessity of creating and maintaining adequate reserves during
profitable years. Reserves are not just nice to have, they are essential and
absolutely required if this, or any Masonic Center, is to remain viable over the
long-term.
What can be done to mitigate the impact of a “down” economy? For
this year and next, your Executive Vice-president, Chris Carpenter, and the
entire Nob Hill staff are engaged in an intense revenue-generating, marketing
and client retention effort. This effort is coupled with a review of each and every
revenue and expense line item, day-by-day, week-by-week, month-by-month and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

329

quarter by quarter, to ensure that revenue goals are obtained and expenses are
held at the lowest practical level through effective budget controls.
th

As reported earlier, this was Nob Hill’s tenth year, and on April 17 ,
we commemorated and celebrated this milestone with a working luncheon for
our Board and an ice cream social for our staff.
We also took this occasion to acknowledge the employee’s years of
service. Four of our eleven employees predate Nob Hill as original employees
of CMMT.
Our Production Manager, Dick Gentschel, has been a part of the team
for 36 years. Dick manned the sound system in 1966 when I had the privilege of
addressing this Grand Lodge as Grand Orator. Dick, Terry Hobson, Don
Darrow and Brad Breece were given special recognition at the celebration for a
combined total of 86 years of service. These four, together, with all the staff,
have earned, and continue to earn and deserve the appreciation of this Grand
Lodge for their dedication, loyalty and commitment.
I opened today’s report with the observation that this current year (July
1, 2002 to June 30, 2003) will most likely be as challenging as 2001/2002. Be
assured that whether that observation is true or not, I am confident, as we should
all be, with regard to the continued success and limitless future for our country,
for our fraternity and for this great Grand Lodge and each of its entities,
including Nob Hill. The future will require our best efforts, and with that effort,
success will be assured.
This is my final report to you, my bretheren, as president of Nob Hill
Masonic Management, Inc. Looking back over the past five years, I am grateful
for all the people who made this experience one of the best of my life. The staff,
has first met and then exceeded every challenge. To them, each and all, must go
full credit and the full measure of my appreciation for making my job both
personally satisfying and richly rewarding. In all honesty, I must confess, that
they, like all good staff, taught me as much, if not more, than I could or did teach
them. Nob Hill continues to be in good hands.
Finally, the Officers and Directors of Nob Hill wish to thank the Grand
Master, C. Ray Whitaker, for the opportunity to serve.
And, Most Worshipful Sir, we would also acknowledge the Junior
Grand Warden, David R. Doan; the Grand Treasurer, Joseph W. R. Ashwill; and
the Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper III for their individual and collective
contributions to our success.

Lodge, our stockholders, for your continued confidence and support.
Respectfully submitted,
