REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON INVESTMENT
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Investment Committee is charged with the prudent handling of
Grand Lodge funds, consisting primarily of the General and Endowment funds
for the California Masonic Homes. We take this charge very seriously. In its
simplest terms, we manage the funds with an eye both for growth and
preservation of capital. We strive to maintain a diversified portfolio that is
designed to generate the necessary funds for the continuing operation of the
Masonic Homes as well as allow it to grow for future needs.
The bear market which officially began in the Spring of 2000 showed
little sign of relenting this past year. The economic recession that was in full
th
bloom before the tragedies of September 11 turned even more severe after.
While our portfolios were not immune to the market downturn, we weathered it
relatively well. Due to our well-diversified portfolio, the Masonic Homes funds
lost a relatively meager 5.2% for the last twelve months ending June 30, 2002.
This compares quite favorably to both our benchmark custom index, which was
down 10.2% and the S&P 500 index, which lost 18% for same period. More
speculative indices such as the NASDAQ have declined more than 75% during
the past two years.
We were able to achieve these favorable results in part because we held
a relatively large portion of the portfolio in fixed income assets and we over
weighted “value” and smaller company stocks whose performance has been
relatively positive of late. This strong relative performance can also be
attributed to the above-benchmark returns by each of our equity managers. In
fact, the returns of our funds have consistently surpassed the performance of the
benchmark since inception, while at the same time incurring an overall lower
level of risk. We believe the portfolio is well balanced going forward, with an
appropriate blend of large and small U.S. stocks (balanced between growth and
value), foreign stocks and bonds.
The market value of the Masonic Homes General and Endowment
portfolios on June 30, 2002 was $555,164,092 compared to $582,550,375 a year
earlier. Please refer to the attachments that follow for a complete performance
summary from each manager for periods covering since inception and by
calendar quarters and years.
In order to fulfill our responsibilities in accordance with the Grand
Lodge Investment Policy and Philosophy, the Investment Committee meets
quarterly with our Investment Consultant, Ennis/Knupp and our portfolio
managers. We review their respective performance compared to various indices
representing their objective. This process provides your Investment Committee

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

337

with the necessary data to properly evaluate each investment manager on a
timely basis, and to observe whether they continue to meet our expectations.
Our committee has worked closely with the Masonic Homes board in
developing a sound policy regarding distributions from the investments. Since
the income produced by the investment portfolio comprises the vast majority of
the Homes’ annual income, a firm understanding between the two groups is
imperative. Ultimately, a “distribution guideline” was established that calls for
the investment portfolio to payout 5% per year based on a five-year rolling
average market value. This strategy is in line with that of other, similar entities
and one that we believe is in the best interest of our constituents.
The Investment Committee also spent considerable time this past year
evaluating all of the investments of the various Grand Lodge entities and
determined that there was considerable opportunity for performance
improvement. To this end, a “master trust arrangement” was developed which
allowed the smaller accounts to ride the “coattails” of the Homes’ portfolios.
The conversion to the master trust was just completed and went very smoothly.
We are also working closely with the new Director of Development,
Doug Ismail, to ensure that donors to the various Grand Lodge charities are
provided the most competitive of investment options.
Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 1 is another product of
the effort the committee expended. The intent of the proposed legislation is to
bring the language of the California Masonic Code in line with modern
investment terminology and options to better protect the assets of the individual
Lodges.
The Committee will be further evaluating potential options for the
Lodge Investment Program and hopes to make the alternatives available to the
Lodges during the first quarter of 2003.
The past year has been challenging from a staffing point of view. Chief
Financial Officer Doug Scott retired early this year and his replacement is still
being sought (as of this writing). Doug was a tremendous asset to the Investment
Committee and to Masons throughout the jurisdiction. While he will be difficult
to replace, the Committee looks forward to working with the new Director of
Investments.
I am grateful for the unflagging dedication of Joseph Ashwill in his role
as Grand Treasurer. He has been instrumental in our recent improvements while
holding that office. I am pleased that he will return to the Committee as a
member following completion of his term as Grand Treasurer.
I also welcome the opportunity to work with Most Worshipful Warren
Blomseth, should he be elected Grand Treasurer. I am confident that his
experience and skill will not only help maintain a successful course, but also
help us progress to even higher levels.

338

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We feel we have a very capable committee consisting of nine brothers
with a combined investment experience in excess of 300 years. My sincere
appreciation is extended to the Grand Lodge Investment Committeemen for the
dedication and devotion they have shown me as Chairman. They have always
been forthright with their opinions and expertise. I am convinced we have the
finest group of investment professionals providing stewardship of this very
important pool of funds.
In summary, while the financial markets have been quite difficult of
late, we are pleased with the results we continue to achieve. As always, there is
much consternation over just what the future holds. Rest assured we remain ever
diligent in our attempts to achieve adequate returns to support the Homes while
at the same time preserving our asset base for future ages.
Respectfully submitted,
Russell E. Charvonia, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.&A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolution. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Whitaker’s decisions
and dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion. In
the Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 31st
of this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents and the
legislation contained in Grand Master Whitaker’s recommendations which he
will present in his message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that
each piece of this legislation has the legal effect set forth in it. There are,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

339

however, three modifications, which we recommend be considered as an
emergent matter.
st

The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 31 of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Whitaker’s recommendations
required no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for
passage of each item before discussion begins. The presiding officer will
announce the vote required for the passage of each item before discussion
begins. A legislation which does not amend the California Masonic Code or the
Ritual requires a simple majority only for adoption. Any amendment to the
California Masonic Code or the Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths
affirmative vote. If the amendment receives a simple majority but less than a
five-sixth affirmative vote, it carries over to the next Annual Communication
when only a two-thirds affirmative vote is required for adoption.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity he has given us to serve
this year.
Respectfully submitted,
John C. Nolan, Chairman
Richard S. Andrews
Ralph C. Shelton, II
Lester W. Miller
Ross A. Simonet
Richard L. Riemer

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC EDUCATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
It has been a rare privilege and honor to serve as chairman of the
Masonic Education Committee for the last five years and a member of the
committee for the past nine. My tenure on the committee expires at the close of
the 2002 Annual Session. I am confident with the abilities of the new Chairman
who has been selected to serve the committee for 2003. Be assured that Masonic
Education in California will continue to expand under his experienced
leadership.
Someone from another jurisdiction recently asked if Masonic education in
California is better now than it was five years ago. I hesitated for a moment then
said it definitely was. There is no question that the programs provided by the
committee have continued to expand during that time. Lodges have far more
educational resources available from Grand Lodge for their candidates and
members than in years past. My hesitation was a concern that not all Lodges are
using these programs or even portions of them to support their own educational
programs. Worse yet there are Lodges who still have no education program at

340

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

all. I have seen an improvement with Lodges becoming more aware of what is
available. That is certainly a marked improvement.
So what are these programs and what has the committee been doing to
provide education for the Lodges in California? They are many and various.
Once again one of the major highlights this past year was the California
Masonic Symposium. Over one hundred and fifty members and their ladies
attended this event. Last year we had just over one hundred. Well-known
Masonic speakers were selected from various parts of the United States. All
comments from the attendees were positive. We are looking forward to the 2003
Masonic Symposium to be held in Los Angles next August. This activity was cohosted by the Grand Lodge Education Committee and the Institute for Masonic
Studies.
The Basic Masonic Education Program for Candidates is growing in its
use. More and more candidates are becoming knowledgeable Masons. The set
of three pamphlets describe primarily the symbols of each degree as well as
providing information pertaining to the history and other aspects of the Grand
Lodge of California. This is now a required Grand Lodge program for
constituent Lodges. Pamphlets were updated last winter and made available to
the Lodges in April of 2002.
The candidate Mentor Program has now been available from Grand
Lodge for a couple of years. Under this program the Master assigns a Mentor to
each applicant. This does not replace the responsibilities of the Candidates
Coach. Ideally the Mentor takes an individual at the time of application and
provides non-ritual instruction through all three degrees and beyond. He
encourages him to explore all facets of Freemasonry.
This past year the committee was invited by the Officer’s Management
committee to be a part of their program. One segment explains the importance of
the Mentor program and how to use it. Other Education Committee segments in
the OMW schedule include, “The History of Masonry prior to the Grand Lodge
of California”, “The History of the Grand Lodge of California” and “Masonic
Symbols”. These sessions have been video taped. Copies of these tapes should
be available to Lodges in 2003.
For over a year the committee has been working with the Grand Lodge
website staff. to provide more Masonic educational resources online. This
includes a portion of the Mentor Manual and the three candidate booklets.
The committee has also been working on a manual for general member
education. Applied Masonry or how one can use the lessons of Masonry in their
daily lives will be the focus of this program. Once this manual is completed it is
possible that an online computer course will be created.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

341

Our Speakers Panel continues to receive requests from Lodges to have
qualified speakers for Lodge events. The main focus of the Speakers Panel is to
provide speakers for Masonic Information Nights. There are a number of
excellent speakers in the north and south.
The Education committee would like to thank those Lodges who have
appointed individuals to assist in promoting educational programs within their
Lodges. It is the responsibility of the Grand Lodge Education Committee to
assist Lodges in establishing sound educational programs. It is also the
responsibility of each Lodge to do what they can to assist in this effort. There are
many resources available today within and without our Jurisdiction to assist
Lodges in their education programs. Ask a member of the Education Committee
for further information.
Respectively submitted,
William N. Wine, Chairman
Bruce H. Abanathie
Guy M. Chalmers

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC HOMES ENDOWMENT
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
On behalf of the Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California
and all the residents in both Union City and Covina, I would like to express my
deepest gratitude to all those who have contributed to the Masonic Homes
Endowment Fund this past year. That being said, it was a very disappointing year
when you look at the results. Our total contributions were $780,937.67; down
from last year’s $859,948.96 or a decrease of 9.2%. Those of our membership
who chose to participate in some fashion decreased 10.3 % from 17,181
members last year to 15,412 members this year.
It is with deeper sadness to think, that although we all profess that it is our
obligation to provide assistance to our Masonic Brothers, their Widows and
Orphans, that only 18.6% of our members cared enough to actually practice what
we preach.
Not all the news is bad though. This was a banner year in disseminating
information out to our membership, as numerous articles were placed in the
“California Freemason”. It was a major goal this past year to ensure that all our
Brothers and Sisters had the right information about what is available to them

342

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

when assistance is required, and to aid them in making a decision whether or not
the Homes were right for them.
Besides these articles, numerous random phone calls were made to
many of our Widows. It was alarming to find the number of widows who knew
nothing about what was available. To this end, next year’s drive will focus on
assistance to our widows. But we as Master Masons must also do a better job in
educating our wives to how they can secure assistance if circumstances require.
A major decision by the Executive Committee was made this past year
to hire a full time fundraiser for all Grand Lodge Entities. This was coupled with
a decision to disband the current Widows Committee and fold them in with the
Homes Endowment Committee. Our charter as a committee will be less fund
raising and more fact finding and information dissemination. Hence, the name of
our committee is also changing to “Fraternal Support Services Committee”. We
will still work to ensure that our membership understands the requirements for
strong donations, but will concentrate on educating our members and their
widows.
We as California Masons can be very proud of the work being
accomplished at both our Homes. Let us all ensure through our donations, that
our Homes stand for another 100 years.
Respectfully Submitted,
Ken G. Nagel, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC SERVICE BUREAUS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus is pleased to submit its
annual report on the operations and conditions of the nine Masonic Service
th
Bureaus for the year ending June 30, 2002. The committee is in its 75 year.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

343

Right Worshipful Howard D. Kirkpatrick, Senior Grand Warden, was
appointed liaison officer to the committee from the Executive Board, and
Worshipful Joel H. Springer III, Assistant Grand Secretary, as the committee
secretary. We appreciate their work with the committee and their support during
the year and at our meetings.
This committee is well aware of its responsibility in overseeing and
directing the operations of the various Bureaus in a uniform manner. The
Service Bureau assists sojourners, their widows and orphans; arrange and
conduct funerals for deceased sojourning Masons; make investigations; assist in
processing applications for our two Masonic Homes and homes in other
jurisdictions; visit the sick; and promote fraternal relations with all sojourners.
Service Bureaus assist all lodges in our jurisdiction when asked for assistance
and assists the Masonic Service Association in the continuing development of its
“Hospital Visitation Program” at several of the Veterans’ Hospital Facilities in
California.
Committee Members
The committee has changed this year and has worked well together in
overseeing their areas of responsibility, to maintain high standards in the
Bureaus of their areas. We are saddened to report the loss of Worshipful John P.
Gibson who passed away on September 14, 2001, at which time Worshipful
Allen S. Beddoe, Executive Secretary of the San Diego Bureau, was appointed
to fill Brother Gibson’s place on the committee. Also, Worshipful Alexander T.
Kirkpatrick went off the committee because of downsizing.
Executive Secretaries
The committee would like to thank all the Executive Secretaries for
their full support this year in maintaining the high standards in each of their
bureaus, as well as getting all their reports in on time as required as part of their
responsibility. It makes for a much smoother operation.
Meetings
We got together at Grand Lodge and had a display with the Department
of Veterans’ Affairs for all our members who are Veterans so they could sign up
for Veterans benefits. We also had a meeting in South Pasadena with all the
Bureaus to go over plans and any problems they had. We also attended the Los
Angeles Masonic Service Bureaus’ Annual Breakfast to honor our Grand
Master.

344

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Bush Masonic Relief
Our thanks to the Bush Masonic Relief for their funding for the three
Service Bureaus (Los Angeles, Long Beach and San Gabriel Valley) in the
greater Los Angeles area. These funds provide a majority of the funds for these
Bureaus to operate. These funds come from the overseeing of the funds by The
Bush Masonic Relief Board. We would like to thank them for their efforts.
Operating Expenses
Total cost for operating the nine bureaus for the year ending June 30,
2002 amounted to $142,309.62, which is $6,351.44 below planned budget.
Some Executive Secretaries receive a modest salary while others receive no
salary. A few choose to receive mileage only.
Conclusions
This committee wishes to thank Worshipful Joel H. Springer III,
Assistant Grand Secretary for all the efforts and support he has given this
committee during the year and his continued leadership and help in the San
Francisco area. This committee sincerely appreciates the support it has received
from the Most Worshipful C. Ray Whitaker, Grand Master and his Grand Lodge
Office staff for their continued support, and to the Grand Secretary, Very
Worshipful John L. Cooper III for his guidance during the year.
Appreciation is also expressed to the Presidents, officers and
representatives of all the Bureaus in this grand jurisdiction for their great and
continued interest and devotion to the activities of the bureaus and for their help
to our sojourning Masons and their families.
Our Executive Secretaries deserve special note of thanks, as they are in
the forefront of our fraternal and charitable business and are on call every day
and night of the year. These men are the backbone of our Bureaus and to each
we express our deep and abiding appreciation for their efforts in doing an
outstanding job.
The committee budget during the past year was sufficient for us to
function properly and a budget of $1,500.00 has been requested for the coming
year.
Respectfully submitted,
A. Bruce Ivie, Chairman
Allen S. Beddoe

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMBERSHIP DEVELOPMENT
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California
During this past Grand Lodge year your committee has processed over
500 requests for assistance or information received from the various Grand
Lodge websites. The requests were answered by a member of the committee or a
member of a local Masonic lodge. We do not have an accurate count of how
many of these requests have resulted in an application for membership. The best
analysis of the activities can be obtained by comparing the number of initiations
conferred in 2001 to the number conferred in 2002.
The list of sojourners from other jurisdictions were distributed to the
Membership Development Committeemen, who in turn delivered them to the
lodges in their area. Several lodges pursued this program with negligible results.
There were several presentations made to Officer’s Schools of
Instruction and to individual lodges concerning the Friend-to-Friend Program,
the Sojourners Program, and the Web Site activities.
Respectfully submitted,
Paul E. McCleary, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMORIAL SERVICE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
"Leaves have their time to fall, and flowers to wither, and stars to set."
And so too, Freemasons have their time to lay down their trowel.
My brothers, since our last meeting here one year ago, three hundred
and forty-five members of this Grand Lodge have been called from labor within
this, our terrestrial lodge.
Let us pray for them.
Supreme Grand Master, Ruler of Heaven and Earth, we pray
for your light and guidance as we remember our dearly
departed brethren. We pray that with Your loving protection
they will know only joy and love as they enter unto eternity.
Please, Oh Lord of Mercy, we beseech thee, keep them and
protect them now and forever more. Amen.
While we have not time enough to eulogize each dear brother in any
detail, we know that if they lived their lives, as we believe they did, influenced
by the pure principles of our tender Craft, the world they left behind is a better
one.
Yea,
"A Mason sleeps in quiet peace,
His every task is done.
He always walked with upright step
'Til his reward was won.
With apron down he faced his work
With kindly lasting smile;
But now he rests in heaven's realm
But we are here awhile.
To emulate his thorough work
Until we greet him where
The Day divided in three parts
Is always kind and fair."
Now is a time to look forward my brothers, as we are taught, look
forward to the acacia as we remember our Brethren who have died. And in
doing so, we are reminded of that immortal part of the spirit or soul of man
which will live when time shall be no more.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

351

Our faith is ever important at this time. That undiscovered country to
which they have embarked has sent no confirmation of their safe arrival. But we
must go forward, seeking more light till we, ourselves, at the Supreme Grand
Master's Word, shall be raised to join our brethren in that blissful lodge above.
Until then my brothers, we must have faith that indeed, the kind
messenger has been sent to translate them from this imperfect to that all perfect
glorious and celestial lodge above where the Supreme Grand Master of the
Universe forever presides.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Chairman
V. Kent Armstrong, Jr.
Larry W. Coyle

Lester W. Miller
Mark Sandstrom

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON OFFICERS' MANAGEMENT WORKSHOPS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Your Officers’ Management Workshop Committee completed a
successful year. Six Workshop presentations were held in San Diego, Santa
Monica, Bakersfield, Petaluma, Modesto and San Bernardino.
Target Audience
The Lodge’s appointed Junior Officers: the Marshal, Junior and Senior
Stewards, Junior and Senior Deacons and prospective officers with no previous
leadership experience.
Purpose
Introduce useful, basic leadership topics about Freemasonry and
organizations of men in general prior to attendance at the Wardens’ Retreats.
Our Mission
The Officers’ Management Workshop Team provides Lodges with
leadership training programs for Junior Officers (the Marshal, Stewards, and
Wardens) and prospective officers.

352

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Our Vision
Effective Masonic Leaders through Leadership Training;
Involved, Enjoyable Lodges from Effective Masonic Leaders;
Better Communities from Involved, Enjoyable Lodges.
Committee History
The current OMW concept began its life in 1996 as a proposed Grand
Lodge committee to encourage and help with the communication of successful
Lodge programming ideas and organization procedures among Lodges
throughout the state. This concept is now a secondary outcome of our primary
emphasis, basic leadership skills training.
This change in priority came as a result of discussions among Stan
Cazneaux, John Cooper and others concerning the time “wasted” during the
Wardens’ Leadership Retreats covering basic organization skills for men whose
participation as a leader in their Masonic Lodge was their first such experience.
Stan and John proposed that we shift our training emphasis to these basic skills
and thereby free the Retreats to “use” the time previously spent teaching basic
concepts in a more creative fashion for solving Lodge problems.
Our early thoughts on the subject of the venues for training included
only four Workshops each year. In our first two meetings during the summer of
1997, we tested the initial ideas for the Agenda and the format.
We intended to do one workshop in each Division and each quarter.
We intended to hold the meetings in a single large audience format. These
efforts met with mixed responses and were significant learning experiences for
us.
Next, our discussions with the Grand Secretary opened the possibility
for the six joint presentations. It seemed to us that the use of shared
infrastructure made sense and in this manner we conducted the second and third
years of the Workshops. During those two years we were constantly changing
presentation agendas and the lesson plans to find those of interest and value to
our participants.
Holding six presentations allowed for sites that were within no more
than two hours’ driving time for most California Freemasons. Two hours seems
to be about the most which men are willing to drive for a one-day program.
Beginning and ending the day in one large group, while holding all other
presentations in three smaller breakout rooms, was also a winner for the
attendees and the presenters. Volunteer presenters do much better in more
intimate settings and the attendees are much more willing to interject ideas and
ask questions in smaller groups.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

353

The success of the current format allowed us to turn to the next step, the
standardization of the lesson plans. Standardization provided us with a set of
presentation topics in outline form and also in a short prose document for
distribution. As the program matured and became used and organized by more
and more people, such standardization provided a sound, approved method of
providing a set of teaching points with which any presenter could convey a
standard set of ideas, but in his own style.
Another outcome from our experiences with the Workshops was the
obvious contradiction between presenting “basic” instruction of many topics in a
short time interval and the desire of both participants and presenters to do a
“full” job of learning and presenting the lesson plans. Presenting nineteen topics
in one day left little time for each. We began using longer presentations and
therefore used fewer lesson plans for each Workshop. We then began a cycle of
the nineteen lesson plans through three years. We planned on presenting about
six per year.
After a couple of years, we determined that there were some presenters
whose expertise lay in only one lesson plan and who would therefore only see
duty every three years. We then decided to keep them fresh and active by
presenting each of the three groups of six lesson plans during two of the
Workshops each year. This has been very successful. Each year’s Workshop in
a given geographical area will use a different lesson plan group than those
presented the year prior (see schedule below).
And, finally, after five (5!) years of presenting Workshops, a bolt from
the blue occurred and we discovered how much easier it would be if we had the
presenters move between the three breakout rooms instead of having all
participants move between the rooms! This has been a very successful, though
belated, “innovation”.
Repetition of the Teaching Modules
Experience in education has shown that repetition is one key to
learning.
We have intentionally chosen topics that can be developed
sequentially for the participants in the Workshops and in the Wardens’
Leadership Retreats. The topics are classified into several “strands” of thought.
Six such strands have been identified; they include Masonic Education, Financial
Management, Membership Development, Program Development, Organizational
Structure and Community Involvement.
The topics within these strands will be usefully developed as a Masonic
leader participates in the several Grand Lodge leadership-training programs in
the following way.
Using a Masonic analogy, the introduction of a topic at the basic level
of the Officers’ Management Workshop is seen as the creation of an ashlar in a

354

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

quarry by an entered apprentice. Learning to do this is a basic skill, which
creates a necessary building block for others to use creatively.
Following the metaphor, the principles of the use of these building
blocks are taught to the Junior Wardens at the Junior Wardens’ Leadership
Retreats. There they are shown how the builder integrates the many necessary
building blocks to form a useful structure, or, in this case, an organization of
men.
Finally, the use of these building blocks for the creative benefit of the
Master’s unique Lodge is seen as the subject of instruction at the Senior
Wardens’ Leadership Retreats. The Senior Warden is pictured as an architect
who manipulates these basic building blocks by the understanding of how they
fit into a building to ultimately create a specific building, or Lodge, which meets
the needs of the members of his specific Lodge.
The following is the current list of teaching strands, their subtopics and
individual lesson plans:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

355

Lesson Plan
Presentation Groups
A
B

The Three-Year Site Cycle
We have received with our OMW feedback surveys perceptions of the
participants concerning OMW sites and marketing. Lodge members desire to be
served by “Grand Lodge” as close to home as possible and they are interested in
attending annual OMW presentations that are “new, not the same as last year”.
We have nineteen topics to cover. We can’t present all of them at each
OMW. We have segmented the presentations into three groupings. Each is
presented twice per year. We do not present the same group in the same
geographical area the next year. We cycle the three in the same geographical
area over a three-year period. Only a few important topics will repeat each year.
This allows the program to be perceived as “new” each year during the cycle.
Also, we shift the presenters of a given topic each year and, because of their
different styles and experiences, the topic becomes “new”.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

357

Below is a schedule to provide an annual OMW in specific
geographical areas of the state and at the same time hold them in different lodges
during a three-year cycle. This will address the two concerns noted in the first
paragraph. The actual dates and sites for these will be set, as the then Grand
Master’s schedule becomes known. We assume, too, that the Wardens’
Leadership Retreats will, in future, be presented using the same schedule as in
previous years. We will endeavor to schedule OMW’s on the opposite side of
the state and not within two weeks of the Retreats, if possible.
Below is a site cycle for six areas around the state. They are organized
in cohorts as follows:

The OMW Team
The OMW Team consists of brethren who are officially posted to the
committee as well as those who participate because of their individual talents
and interests. The following constituted the OMW Team this year: Bruce
Abanathie, John Adams, Larry Adamson, Russ Charvonia, Ron Cooper, Gene
Goldman, Kim Hegg, Lee Hughes, Rick Hutchinson, Levon Keshishian, Ron
Koretz, Paul McCleary, Tom McKinney, Scott Nave, Mark Sandstrom, Hal
Scott, Dan Sola, Joel Springer, Jim Sword, Gloria Vlachos and Bill Wine.
Rick Hutchinson, author of this report, ends his official five-year tenure
as chairman of your committee. He is the last of the originating team to leave.
He has the utmost respect and admiration for all past and current members of the
OMW Team. He thanks everyone involved, especially the members of the
Executive Committee during his tenure, for their willingness to dream, to plan, to
act, to boldly assess outcomes, to give and receive criticism, to change, to endure
and to give of themselves unfailingly! Because of them, your Grand Lodge
Leadership Training Programs are not only a vital benefit to the members who
use them, but also for the future strength of Freemasonry in California.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON PUBLIC SCHOOLS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
This report incorporates and summarizes the Quarterly Reports given at
the California Masonic Foundation Meetings and made a part of their records, as
well as outlines how we can meet our charge as a Committee, as set forth in
Section 6060 of the California Masonic Code and as defined in the Grand Lodge
Roster.
During the past year a new edition of the Public Schools Support
Guidelines, prepared by my predecessor Worshipful Roy Youngblood, and a
letter from Worshipful Douglas Cairns outlining four programs that have worked
in the Fresno-Clovis area were sent to all Lodges. In addition, Delaine Easton,
State Superintendent of Schools, sent a letter to all County and District
Superintendents in the State of California, acknowledging the Craft’s continuing
commitment to and support of Public Schools. It was gratifying to see
recognition by the schools on a State level after many years of our failing to
work together. Other areas of outreach during this year included an article by
the Chair in the April issue of the California Freemason and his leadership at
Officers Management Workshops in Petaluma and Santa Monica.
I am pleased to say that a very large number of our Lodges have
effective programs in place with their local schools, and such successes as have
been accomplished have been truly done at the local level.
There has been one Committee meeting, at the Masonic Home in
Covina, and I would like to have one at Grand Lodge and one again in Southern
California in November. The Committee has been in contact through e-mail,
letter, telephone, and fax. I am happy to report that we have a number of
members who are quite effective in their efforts and wish to be more so. This
year has been a good start in outreach and rebuilding infrastructure, but much
remains to be done.
It is my opinion that we should have a better geographic spread on the
committee and strive to have a committeeman within a two-hour drive of any
Lodge in the State. With the continuing mergers and cessation of our Lodges,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

359

more school districts find themselves without a local Lodge to offer support. If
these districts are to avoid “falling through the cracks”, we should consider
supporting them through the Grand Lodge Public Schools Committee. There are
many programs we can implement to show our support which have a minimal
cost and yet can be of great benefit.
In summary, progress has been made. More remains to be done,
especially in the areas of building infrastructure, outreach, and support of local
Lodges and School Districts who have no Lodge nearby.
Respectfully submitted,
James A. Sword, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
RESEARCH AND PLANNING
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California:
Five years ago the then leadership of this Grand Lodge identified the
need to formally address a more consistent and continued strategic planning
process for our jurisdiction. The committee’s purpose was to “Conduct research,
evaluate alternatives and make recommendations on issues impacting
Freemasonry in California”. The committee under the then forward leadership
of Most Worshipful, E. Arthur Haglund prepared and delivered a strategic plan
that has guided the leadership of this Grand Lodge and continues to be as
strategic today as it was when it was conceived. To Most Worshipful Haglund
and his founding committee we are indebted.
During this past year, the Research and Planning Committee addressed
a multitude of issues and I’m please that several of the recommendations and
resolutions that you will be asked to consider later this afternoon and tomorrow
are the results of the committee efforts to address emerging issues in
Freemasonry.
The past year Research and Planning undertook and studied a number
of issues:

360

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We studied the relationship between Grand Lodges and their constituent
lodges as it related to balancing ritualistic and management needs. That study
resulted in the outline of an alternative Grand Lodge Administrative
Organizational Structure, and it was submitted to the executive committee and is
now being finalized for consideration in this upcoming Masonic Year. Working
with the Executive Committee we addressed the current Grand Lodge Committee
Structure which resulted in Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 2.
At the request of the Executive Committee, after hearing concerns from many
lodge leaders regarding a cumbersome process for address changes to the Lodge
dues in light of increasing Per Capita and diminished net proceeds to the lodge,
Research and Planning drafted an support Resolution No. 02-01 that will be
before you tomorrow. Under membership considerable time and research was
expended to address the trends and outcomes of Grand Jurisdictions that are
utilizing accelerated degrees or One-day Class formats. The conclusion of this
very important research is scheduled to conclude this next year and formal
recommendations are likely to be put before you at next year’s communication.
Tied to the larger issue of membership and candidate advancement the
committee worked with our Grand Master in surveying our non-advancing
entered apprentices and fellow-crafts. Their responses are an essential
component to the development of meaningful change.
Additionally, we were working with the Ritual Committee and the
feasibility of enhancing degree lectures with multi-media support. A concept
that purports to have had good results in improving member understanding of
Masonic principals in several other Grand Jurisdictions.
In addition to those continuing research topics future study priorities
will include the possible restructuring the content of the Annual Communication.
Looking at emphasis on Masonic Education and leadership training, degree
exemplification and even the timing and whether a beginning weekend starting
time may be more accommodating. And most importantly this year marks the
final year of the five-year strategic plan and the committee has been asked to
support the Executive Committee as it looks to move that planning effort
forward for the next five years.
Most Worshipful, I would be derelict if I didn’t acknowledge the effort
of those who served this committee. To the Deputy Grand Master, Senior and
Junior Grand Wardens and to Most Worshipful Jack Levitt, Fred Sorsabal and
Mel Stein and the craft, Thank you for your strategic wisdom and undying
support.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

361

And to you Most Worshipful thank you for allowing me to be of
service.
Respectfully submitted,
Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
M. William Holsinger
Howard Kirkpatrick

Frederick L. Sorsabal
Melvyn B. Stein
Jack R. Levitt, PGM

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON TRIAL REVIEW
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Since the 2001 Annual Communication there have been referred to your
Committee on Trial Review nine completed trials and two applications for
restoration from expulsion. In addition, there were three cases which were
disposed of before trial and nine cases pending but not completed and submitted
to Grand Lodge for review.
Your Committee has reviewed each of the Trial Records numbered
2448, 2441, 2442, 2443, 2444, 2449, 2445, 2450 and 2447 and finds that in each
case there was substantial compliance with the procedural requirements of our
Constitution and Ordinances and the result is supported in the record of each
case, and there are no miscarriages of justice.
Your Committee therefore submits and moves the adoption of the
following resolution:
“Resolved that in each of the Trial Records numbered 2448, 2441,
2442, 2443, 2444, 2449, 2445 and 2447 the judgment and penalty
imposed be ratified, affirmed and approved.”
Your Committee thanks the various Inspectors, the Grand Secretary and
his staff, as well as the Grand Master for their efforts to resolve these conflicts
without a Masonic Trial. Each of us again should be reminded of our
obligations as Masons to whisper wise counsel in the ear of an offending brother
instead of being eager to rush forward with Masonic charges. If peace and
harmony is to prevail in our lodges, each of us must practice our Masonic tenets
on a daily basis.
We again, as a reminder, encourage Past Masters to consider becoming
Trial Masters. The job is critical to the well-being of our craft and there is
training and a manual available to guide you through the process.

362

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Most Worshipful Grand Master, we have two applications for
restoration to report on separately.
Trial Record No. 1994
This is an Application for Restoration from Expulsion. The accused, a
former member of Oak Grove Lodge No. 215 (now Island City Lodge No. 215),
was found guilty of unmasonic conduct, fraud, deceit and grand theft. He was
found guilty in his Masonic Trial. The Trial Master fixed the penalty at
Expulsion.
He now appeals to Grand Lodge for restoration. His Application for
Restoration was voted on by his lodge at its October 4, 2002 stated meeting.
The records provided by the Lodge Secretary indicate that there were twentyfour members present and no letters of character recommendation were
presented.
Your Committee on Trial Review has reviewed the entire record of this
matter, including the Application for Restoration. Taking into consideration the
entire record and the seriousness of the offense and the Application for
Restoration, it is the recommendation of your Committee on Trial Review due to
the absence of any character references that the Application for Restoration be
denied. Therefore, we submit and move the adoption of the following
resolution:
“Resolved that the Application for Restoration for Trial Record No.
1994 be denied.”
Recommendation ADOPTED.
Trial Record No. 2394
This is an Application for Restoration from Expulsion. The accused, a
former member of Los Angeles Harbor Lodge No. 332, was found guilty of
unmasonic conduct, fraud and deceit. He was found guilty in his Masonic Trial.
The Trial Master fixed the penalty at Expulsion.
He now appeals to Grand Lodge for restoration. His Application of
Restoration was voted on by his lodge at its September stated meeting. The
records provided by the Ldoge Secretary indicate that there were only twenty
members present and three character letters presented.
Your Committee on Trial Review has reviewed the entire record of this
matter, including the attached letters of recommendation and the Application for
Restoration. Taking into consideration the entire record and the seriousness of
the offense and the Application for Restoration, it is the recommendation of your
Committee on Trial Review that the Application for Restoration be denied.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

363

Therefore, we submit and move the adoption of the following resolution:
“Resolved that the Application for Restoration for Trial Record No.
2394 be denied.”
Recommendation ADOPTED.
Respectfully submitted,

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON U.S. CONSTITUTION OBSERVANCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
This year the activities of the U.S. Constitution Observance Committee
has enjoyed an increase in participation by the individual Lodges throughout the
State of California. Requests for Certificates of Participation have increased
from 12 to over 70 with many reports still to come at the time of this writing.
There is increased evidence of cooperation between a number of
Lodges to enlarge their Constitution Observance and garner a greater awareness
and recognition by the community as a result. Norwalk Lodge No. 315
continues to spearhead a program in Southern California involving nearly a
dozen Lodges, held at the Independence Hall replica at Knott’s Berry Farm. San
Jose Lodge No. 10 has an admirable combined program as does Washington
Lodge No. 20, Nevada Ctiy, Newhall, San Luis Obispo and many more.
This year, the Grand Lodge session will feature a huge Patriotic
Extravaganza as its featured Monday Night Entertainment, replete with waving
flags and period re-enactors in authentic costume.
Over 40 Lodges are now utilizing materials from the National
Constitution Center in Philadelphia and are availing themselves of Constitution
booklets at a very low cost to distribute to children and adults as part of their
year long activities. This organization provides plans, guidelines, news releases
and materials for distribution at a minimal cost. These “I Signed the
Constitution” programs are very successful and popular with the community.
Also this year, the Committee enlarged the scope of its participation to
include all patriotic holidays, including Memorial Day, Flag Day, Independence
Day and Veterans’ Day and encouraged the individual Lodges to take part in
civic celebrations of these holidays, as well as part of their Constitutional

364

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Observance obligation. This appears to be a popular addition to the Constitution
Observance program and I am receiving a great deal of favorable response.
The thrust of the committee this next year will be to encourage local
Lodges to distribute Constitution materials to fifth grade students in their area,
much like the Grand Lodge of Alabama.
th

September 11 has made the whole Nation aware of the value of its
heritage. This is an excellent opportunity for us, the Freemasons of California,
to lead the parade of Patriotism back into our communities and inspire our fellow
citizens by our example.
Respectfully submitted,
B. Eugene Collins, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
WARDENS’ LEADERSHIP RETREATS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Warden’s Leadership Program continues to be a highly successful
Grand Lodge program, which provides training for the Senior and Junior
Wardens in leadership techniques. This year we had 166 Senior Wardens and
103 of their ladies, 155 Junior Wardens and 89 of their ladies. Again this year,
the attendance was lower than in the past. We are reaching less than 50% of our
Lodges in this endeavor. We must renew our commitment to providing the best
possible leadership-training program so that our Lodges have well trained
leaders as Masters of our Lodges. This program is a culmination of the
leadership program provided by Grand Lodge.
The following is a breakdown of the Retreats:
Senior Wardens’, North - 81 Wardens and 51 ladies
March 1 –3, 2002, San Ramon Marriott Hotel at Bishop Ranch
Senior Wardens’, South - 85 Wardens and 52 ladies
March 15 –17, 2002, Crowne Plaza Hotel at LAX Airport

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

365

Junior Wardens’ – South – 85 Wardens and 47 ladies
May 3 – 5, 2002, Crowne Plaza Hotel at LAX Airport
Senior Wardens’, North
- 70 Wardens and 42 ladies
May 17 – 18, 2002, San Ramon Marriott Hotel at Bishop Ranch
Contracts have been signed with the hotels until 2007 and the Wardens
Leadership Program has major commitments for rooms and meals along with
program content and it is imperative that we increase attendance at these
sessions. It is a great opportunity to upgrade the leadership skills of the officers
of our various Lodges.
The Senior Wardens’ Leadership Program was under the direction of
our Deputy Grand Master, William Holsinger and his wife Jean. The Retreats
were built around the theme for the 2003 Masonic Year – ”Freemasonry-Seeks
good men and makes them better. Tell a friend”. Leadership training for this
session centered on the book and video of Who Moved My Cheese by Spencer
Johnson, MD. Along with sessions on planning, communications, finance and
budgeting, building the lodge team, the Annual Communication, and
membership development. These subjects were reinforced through the use of
Team Building Exercises and Group Reports. The ladies participated in a
number of these sessions and group exercises, which proved very lively.
The Junior Wardens’ Leadership Program was under the direction of
our Senior Grand Warden, Howard Kirkpatrick and his wife Jackie with the
theme “Personal Responsibility – Accept it and Teach It”. These sessions were
centered on the discovery of “social skills” and how to identify and deal with
different people. Included in these sessions were program planning, finance and
investments, Masonic Education and membership development, the California
Masonic Code and how to use it, the Masonic Home, and how to take home what
you learn.
As in the past several years, the Executive Committee of Grand Lodge
participated greatly in the sessions along with Don Nielsen, Allan Casalou, John
Nolan, Debi Stebbins, Barbara Tenbrook, Russ Charvonia, Norm Booi, John
Cooper, Larry Adamson, and Loyal Stark. The wives of the Grand Lodge
Officers along with Marlene Sorsabal, Lynn Adamson, and Kim Hegg provided
the leadership for the lady’s sessions. Each of the facilitators did an outstanding
job and was well received in both the men and women’s sessions.
Again this year, a very special thanks is extended to Kim Hegg of the
Grand Lodge office staff for the outstanding support and participation she has
provided. The amount of extra work Kim does to make these programs
successful is extraordinary. She provides the liaison between the committee and

366

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the attendees and all necessary materials for the program. Without her help, the
success of the program would not be what it is.
As this is Loyal Starks’ last year to participate in the Leadership
Program, I want to express my grateful appreciation for his enthusiasm,
participation, and advice. Loyal has been a tremendous asset to this program
and will be greatly missed. I want to thank the Grand Lodge Officers for their
commitment and support of this program. They know that without this
leadership program, our Lodges would not have the background for their leaders
to be successful.
As this is my last year as Chairman of the Warden’s Leadership
Program, I want to thank the staff at Grand Lodge for their continued support.
Along with Kim Hegg; Gloria Vlachos, Staff Accountant, Linda Ross and Terrie
Strokos in the Supply Room have been outstanding workers in our endeavors. I
would also like to express my thanks to Allan Casalou for his outstanding
participation in this program. He is greatly appreciated by the participants and
very knowledgeable in training and without his advice and counsel, these
programs would not be successful.
Lastly, I would like to thank my wife Marlene for her continued
assistance and long hours of work for these Retreats. Her enthusiasm and skills
provides the opportunity to coordinate the sessions in a very special way. Her
support and understanding of our fraternity is outstanding and greatly
appreciated.
Respectfully submitted,

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON YOUTH ORDERS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M.of California:
For the last two Masonic years, the Youth Orders Committee has had
discussions on how to better serve the Grand Lodge and the Youth Orders of this
Jurisdiction. A new Mission Statement, with a set of goals was agreed upon and
adopted. It is as follows:
The mission of the California Masonic Youth Orders Committee is to
inspire, within each Lodge a desire by its members to support our Youth Orders
(International Order of Job's Daughters, International Order of Rainbow for Girls
and The International Order of DeMolay).
Goals:
1.

To communicate with the Grand Master and obtain permission to
speak at the Wardens Retreats and Officer Workshops.

2.

To identify each Masonic Youth Order and the Masonic Lodge that
supports that group(s).

3.

To communicate with the Inspector of each District and the Master
of each and offer our assistance in accomplishing our Mission, and
to identify prospective adult leaders to recommend to the local
Chapter, Assembly and Bethel.

4.

To communicate with each of the Assemblies, Chapters and
Bethels to determine the level of attendance and support of the
Masonic Fraternity.

5.

To communicate the status of the Masonic Support of our Youth
Orders to the Grand Masters.

6.

To assist the Grand Master in recognizing those Brethren and/or
Lodges which exemplified the epitome of support for a Masonic
Youth Order(s).

(Note: The Mission Statement will remain the same from year to year,
but the Goals may be changed or added to.)
The Youth Orders Committee held three meetings this Masonic year,
two at the Masonic Home in Covina in February and July, and a meeting at the
beginning of the Annual Communication. The Chairman or his representative
attended the NorCal DeMolay Conclave, Rainbow Grand Assembly, Grand
Bethel and the SoCal DeMolay Conclave. The Chairman also hosted the
Masonic Adult Youth Orders meeting between the Grand Master with his

368

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

executive committee and the Adult Leaders of the Youth Orders.
Your Committee on Youth Orders respectfully submits this report on its
activities for the 2002 Masonic year. We thank you for the opportunity to have
served this Grand Lodge and its elected and appointed officers, our constituent
Lodges and our Masonic Youth Orders.
Respectfully submitted,
Raymond H. Daley, Chairman
Richard W. Jeanson, Vice Chairman

NAVIGATION TIPS

Scroll through the document using
scroll bars on the side of the window or
the page navigation tool at the bottom.

Click on any of the items in the bookmark
section of the document and you’ll navigate
directly to that page or section.
You can then move to the next page using the
ARROW KEYS on your keyboard.
Return to the beginning of the document at any
time by clicking on the Table of Contents
Bookmark.
If you want to enlarge or reduce the size of the
document, click on the Zoom In/Zoom Out
bookmarks.
Navigate through the document
by clicking on any of the items in
the Table of Contents or the
Index.
The “Hand Tool” will change to
an “Index Finger”…click on the
line and you’ll move to the
desired page.
To get back, click on the Table
of Contents/Index bookmarks.
